Electricians' Workplace English

Clarify electrical service reports, lighting specifications, device positions, trade interfaces, planned interruptions, technical findings, and handover records.

For Electricians, electrical apprentices in supervised communication roles, electrical crew leads, and service coordinators.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Narrow a broad client description to named devices, location, and observation time without inferring technical status or requesting tests.
  • Explain the difference between light output and color temperature while preserving unresolved compatibility and substitution decisions.
  • Clarify the datum and device reference behind a mounting dimension without treating another trade drawing as installation approval.
  • Describe a cross-trade drawing conflict, request joint review, and preserve the distinction between a meeting and an approved route.
  • Preserve the proposed scope and time window, correct an overstated notice, and distinguish facilities approval from electrical status.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Your eight lessons

Open the learner book (PDF) ↗
01 Taking a precise electrical service request02 Clarifying lighting specifications03 Agreeing device positions and drawing references04 Coordinating containment and shared work areas05 Communicating outage plans and status06 Reporting findings and uncertainty07 Assessing added equipment and substitutions08 Handing over electrical records
Prompts

The cases and products are fictional. This book teaches English, not electrical testing, switching, isolation, diagnosis, installation, circuit identification, or code compliance. Follow actual training, authorization, procedures, manufacturer information, and applicable requirements. Client descriptions, labels, outage plans, and handover records do not prove equipment is de-energized or safe to use or work on. These dialogues authorize no electrical operation, relabeling, routing change, substitution, or added load.

01Taking a precise electrical service requestWhich sockets, where, and since when?+

Your goal

Narrow a broad client description to named devices, location, and observation time without inferring technical status or requesting tests.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Which sockets, where, and since when?

Client Rosa tells electrician Kai that the office sockets are dead. Her account concerns two desk receptacles in Room 204, noticed at 08:30. Other areas have not been assessed. A panel-directory entry says office, but its accuracy has not been verified. Kai must clarify the request from the information Rosa already has. He must not ask her to test or operate anything, expand the report to the whole office, or treat dead as proof of safe electrical status.

Rosa: Client / Kai: Electrician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Rosa's account actually cover?

Answer and explanation

A. Two desk receptacles in Room 204

The specific account concerns two desk receptacles in Room 204, not the whole office.

2. What does 08:30 represent?

Answer and explanation

A. When Rosa noticed the problem

The time records Rosa's observation, not the exact onset or an assessment result.

3. What is the status of the panel-directory entry?

Answer and explanation

A. It says office, but accuracy is unverified

The label's wording is known, but its identification accuracy has not been verified.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Dead is Rosa's informal description, not verified de-energized or isolated status. Her account identifies two desk receptacles in Room 204. Other areas are unassessed, so they must not be described as either working normally or affected.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
receptacle
Device providing contacts for a plug connection.identify the reported receptacles
socket
Common term for a receptacle or plug-in connection point.clarify which sockets are reported
outlet
Point where electrical energy is supplied to utilization equipment.identify the outlet location
desk receptacle
Receptacle serving or located at a desk position.specify the desk receptacle
service request
Report asking for attention to a stated issue.clarify the service request
client account
Description supplied by the customer.preserve the client account
reported symptom
Problem described by a person rather than established by a technical finding.record the reported symptom
observation time
When a person noticed the described condition.record the observation time
onset
Time when a condition began, if established.distinguish observation from onset
affected location
Area named in the report as experiencing the issue.specify the affected location
unassessed area
Location whose condition has not been evaluated.identify unassessed areas
panel directory
List identifying circuits or areas associated with a panel.verify the panel directory
directory entry
Individual item in a panel or circuit list.quote the directory entry
circuit identification
Establishing which circuit corresponds to a device or area.refer circuit identification for verification
circuit
Electrical path or connected arrangement through which current may flow.distinguish the circuit from the room name
distribution panel
Assembly distributing electrical supply to outgoing circuits.identify the distribution panel
IP code
Enclosure protection code with separate digits for solid-object and water protection.verify the IP code
room reference
Identifier locating a room in the building.include the room reference
verified status
Condition established through the appropriate authorized process.distinguish verified status
de-energized
In a state without energization, requiring appropriate verification before reliance.avoid an unsupported de-energized claim
isolation status
Established state of separation from relevant energy sources under the actual procedure.report only verified isolation status
technical finding
Conclusion supported by the relevant professional assessment.distinguish a technical finding
scope of report
Extent of the issue actually described by the reporting person.limit the scope of report
readback
Repetition of important information to confirm accuracy.give a precise readback

08:30 is the time the issue was noticed, not a proven onset time. The directory says office, but its accuracy is unverified. Clarify the report from known observations without asking the client to operate, open, switch, reset, or test any equipment.

C Notice the language

Translate broad wording into a precise report

Clients often use a general room name and an everyday word for a problem. Ask neutral questions about what they already observed. Keep their report attributed, preserve unassessed areas as unknown, and avoid turning an informal description into a technical finding.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the report
I will record what you have already observed.
Narrow the location
Which room does your report concern?
Confirm the devices
You mean the two desk receptacles in Room 204.
Clarify the time
Is 08:30 when you first noticed the issue?
Avoid an onset claim
We have not established exactly when it began.
Keep attribution
The client reports that those two receptacles are not working as expected.
Preserve unknown areas
Other areas have not been assessed.
Avoid a whole-office claim
I will not describe this as a verified office-wide outage.
Quote the label accurately
The directory entry says office.
Keep identification open
The directory's accuracy has not been verified.
Separate wording and status
Dead is your description, not verified electrical status.
Avoid a safety inference
I cannot infer that equipment is de-energized or safe from this report.
Use existing observations
You do not need to test or operate anything for this clarification.
Keep the count visible
The report concerns two devices, not an unspecified number.
Read back the request
Two desk receptacles, Room 204, noticed at 08:30, other areas unassessed.
Close the intake
I will preserve the unverified directory entry separately from your device report.
6 language and precision notes
The client reports
Attributes information rather than presenting it as a verified finding.
Noticed at
Describes observation time without proving when the issue began.
Two
Limits the device count instead of generalizing to a whole room or floor.
Dead
Informal wording that must not become a technical statement of de-energization.
Unassessed
Means unknown, not unaffected and not necessarily faulty.
Says versus verifies
A directory label provides wording, not proof of correct circuit identification.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which service summary is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Client reports two desk receptacles in Room 204, noticed at 08:30; other areas unassessed.

The summary preserves the attributed devices, location, observation time, and unassessed areas.

2. Which question is appropriate?

Answer and explanation

A. Which room does your existing report concern?

The question clarifies known information without asking the client to perform an electrical action.

3. What does the label office establish?

Answer and explanation

A. Only the wording of an unverified directory entry

The entry's wording is known, but its accuracy and associated electrical status are not.

4. Which statement wrongly upgrades the evidence?

Answer and explanation

C. The receptacles are confirmed de-energized because the client called them dead.

An informal symptom description does not establish de-energization, isolation, or safety.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

Which sockets, where, and since when?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Rosa: Client / Kai: Electrician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • unassessed areas
  • observation time
  • Circuit identification
  • room reference
  • readback
  • service request
  • reported symptom
  • verified status
  • directory entry
  • receptacles
  1. Rosa
    The office sockets are dead, Kai. What information do you need for the request? I can tell you what we've already noticed.
  2. Kai
    Let us clarify the 1 using what you already know. Which room and which connection points does your report concern? You do not need to test or operate anything.
  3. Rosa
    It's the two desk receptacles in Room 204. When I said office, I meant that room, not every office on the floor.
  4. Kai
    I will use the 2 of 204 and name the two desk receptacles. I will not expand that to every connection point in the office.
  5. Rosa
    I noticed it at eight-thirty. I don't know whether it began then or earlier; that's when I became aware of the problem.
  6. Kai
    Then 08:30 is the 3. I will keep that distinction and avoid saying the fault definitely began at that exact time.
  7. Rosa
    The other rooms haven't been assessed as part of this report. My wording shouldn't suggest they're all affected or all working.
  8. Kai
    I will describe those as 4. That means their status is unknown here, not that they are definitely affected or definitely working normally.
  9. Rosa
    The records we already hold include a directory entry saying office. I don't know whether it correctly identifies these two devices.
  10. Kai
    I will record the 5 separately. Its wording is useful information, but it does not verify which circuit serves the devices in your report.
  11. Rosa
    Should I describe them as receptacles or outlets? I want the actual devices identified, rather than an uncertain whole-room arrangement.
  12. Kai
    We can specify two desk 6. The important detail is the exact devices and room, not replacing your wording with a broader claim about the electrical installation.
  13. Rosa
    By dead I mean they weren't working as I expected. I'm not claiming someone checked their electrical state or isolated them.
  14. Kai
    I will preserve that as a 7. Dead in a client account is not proof that equipment is de-energized, isolated, or safe to use or work on.
  15. Rosa
    That's helpful. I don't want an everyday description copied into the request as if a technical assessment has already been completed.
  16. Kai
    There is no 8 established by this conversation. I am clarifying the information for the request, not reporting a completed electrical assessment.
  17. Rosa
    Could you read back the room, count, and observation time? Those are the details I can confirm from what I already know.
  18. Kai
    The 9 is: client reports two desk receptacles in Room 204, noticed at 08:30; exact onset unknown; other areas unassessed; directory wording unverified.
  19. Rosa
    Yes, that's accurate. Please retain office as unverified wording and keep it separate from the two devices I've reported.
  20. Kai
    I will. 10 remains open for the appropriate process. The request will preserve your observations without asking you to carry out any electrical action or adding a technical finding.

Answers and explanations
  1. service request Turn 2. Service request captures Rosa's existing observations without requiring her to operate or test electrical equipment.
  2. room reference Turn 4. Room reference is Room 204, which is more precise than treating the word office as the entire office area.
  3. observation time Turn 6. Observation time records when Rosa noticed the issue, not a proven start time for the underlying condition.
  4. unassessed areas Turn 8. Unassessed areas remain unknown; the report does not establish that they are either affected or working normally.
  5. directory entry Turn 10. Directory entry supplies the word office, but its accuracy and relationship to the reported devices remain unverified.
  6. receptacles Turn 12. Receptacles identifies the two plug-connection devices in Rosa's account and keeps the device description specific.
  7. reported symptom Turn 14. Reported symptom attributes the informal description to Rosa without treating it as a verified electrical condition.
  8. verified status Turn 16. Verified status is not supplied by this intake conversation, so no assessment or safety conclusion can be claimed.
  9. readback Turn 18. Readback confirms the two devices, Room 204, and the 08:30 observation while retaining unknown conditions elsewhere.
  10. Circuit identification Turn 20. Circuit identification remains unverified; the office label is not proof of which circuit serves the reported receptacles.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Rosa: The office sockets are dead, Kai. What information do you need for the request? I can tell you what we've already noticed.
  2. Kai: Let us clarify the service request using what you already know. Which room and which connection points does your report concern? You do not need to test or operate anything.
  3. Rosa: It's the two desk receptacles in Room 204. When I said office, I meant that room, not every office on the floor.
  4. Kai: I will use the room reference of 204 and name the two desk receptacles. I will not expand that to every connection point in the office.
  5. Rosa: I noticed it at eight-thirty. I don't know whether it began then or earlier; that's when I became aware of the problem.
  6. Kai: Then 08:30 is the observation time. I will keep that distinction and avoid saying the fault definitely began at that exact time.
  7. Rosa: The other rooms haven't been assessed as part of this report. My wording shouldn't suggest they're all affected or all working.
  8. Kai: I will describe those as unassessed areas. That means their status is unknown here, not that they are definitely affected or definitely working normally.
  9. Rosa: The records we already hold include a directory entry saying office. I don't know whether it correctly identifies these two devices.
  10. Kai: I will record the directory entry separately. Its wording is useful information, but it does not verify which circuit serves the devices in your report.
  11. Rosa: Should I describe them as receptacles or outlets? I want the actual devices identified, rather than an uncertain whole-room arrangement.
  12. Kai: We can specify two desk receptacles. The important detail is the exact devices and room, not replacing your wording with a broader claim about the electrical installation.
  13. Rosa: By dead I mean they weren't working as I expected. I'm not claiming someone checked their electrical state or isolated them.
  14. Kai: I will preserve that as a reported symptom. Dead in a client account is not proof that equipment is de-energized, isolated, or safe to use or work on.
  15. Rosa: That's helpful. I don't want an everyday description copied into the request as if a technical assessment has already been completed.
  16. Kai: There is no verified status established by this conversation. I am clarifying the information for the request, not reporting a completed electrical assessment.
  17. Rosa: Could you read back the room, count, and observation time? Those are the details I can confirm from what I already know.
  18. Kai: The readback is: client reports two desk receptacles in Room 204, noticed at 08:30; exact onset unknown; other areas unassessed; directory wording unverified.
  19. Rosa: Yes, that's accurate. Please retain office as unverified wording and keep it separate from the two devices I've reported.
  20. Kai: I will. Circuit identification remains open for the appropriate process. The request will preserve your observations without asking you to carry out any electrical action or adding a technical finding.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Which office sockets?

An electrician clarifies the fictional Room 204 service call by phone.

  1. Electrician: This service call says office sockets. Which room and devices do you mean?
  2. Client: The two desk receptacles in Room 204. I noticed the problem at eight thirty.
  3. Electrician: Thank you. Have you already observed anything elsewhere, without doing any further checks?
  4. Client: No, I only know about those two. Someone mentioned the whole floor.
  5. Electrician: I'll keep that broader suggestion separate from what you actually reported to us.
  6. Client: The distribution board's directory says office. Doesn't that confirm the circuit identifier?
  7. Electrician: It gives us a reference, but the circuit identification still needs verification.
  8. Client: Should I send you a photograph of the room label from the corridor?
  9. Electrician: Yes, if it's available without approaching equipment; no electrical investigation is needed from you.
  10. Client: I'll send that existing photograph and leave the service description limited to my observations.
Outlet means different things to different people

An electrician clarifies a shop manager's request about a wall device.

  1. Electrician: You asked us to relocate an outlet. Which item on this elevation do you mean?
  2. Shop manager: The round fitting above the display, where the light comes out near the sign.
  3. Electrician: That's a light fitting rather than a receptacle. Are you requesting a position change?
  4. Shop manager: Yes, the new sign would cover it. I used outlet because I couldn't name it.
  5. Electrician: No problem. We'll identify the fitting by its drawing tag so the request is clear.
  6. Shop manager: The tag is L7, and the sign company has a revised elevation available.
  7. Electrician: Please send that elevation with the request; a photograph alone won't define the new position.
  8. Shop manager: Can you price it now, or do you need the proposed location first?
  9. Electrician: We need the location and a review of the affected work before quoting.
  10. Shop manager: Then I'll obtain the elevation and describe this as relocating fitting L7, not a socket.
Yesterday's account is secondhand

An electrician checks the source of an intermittent complaint with a caretaker.

  1. Electrician: The service note says the lobby lights flickered yesterday. Did you see that yourself?
  2. Caretaker: No, the evening receptionist told me. I wasn't on site during that shift.
  3. Electrician: Do you know the time or which fittings they were describing in the lobby?
  4. Caretaker: They said around six, near the reception counter, but I don't have exact identifiers.
  5. Electrician: I'll record those details as their report and keep the locations approximate for now.
  6. Caretaker: Would it help if I asked them to clarify what they already noticed?
  7. Electrician: Yes, ask about the time and location, not for them to reproduce the issue.
  8. Caretaker: Understood. I'll also check whether there is an earlier service reference for those fittings.
  9. Electrician: That would help connect the history without assuming the same cause has returned.
  10. Caretaker: I'll send the reference if I find it, clearly separate from yesterday's secondhand account.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Which sockets, where, and since when?
  1. Read turns 1-10, stressing two, Room 204, and noticed at 08:30.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20; keep dead attributed to the client, not a verified electrical state.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Keep the report attributed and specific

Complete the intake readback from the client information. Do not turn the word dead or an unverified label into a technical conclusion.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Electrician: "Your report concerns ___ desk receptacles."

Answer and explanation

C. two

Two is the device count in the client's specific account.

2. Client: "They are in Room ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 204

Room 204 identifies the reported location without including the whole office.

3. Electrician: "You noticed the issue at ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 08:30

This records the observation time rather than a verified onset time.

4. Client: "The directory entry remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unverified

The word office appears in the directory, but its accuracy is unconfirmed.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Follow actual training, authorization, procedures, manufacturer information, and applicable requirements. Lesson 01: Taking a precise electrical service request
Original fictional case: Client Rosa tells electrician Kai that the office sockets are dead. Her account concerns two desk receptacles in Room 204, noticed at 08:30. Other areas have not been assessed. A panel-directory entry says office, but its accuracy has not been verified. Kai must clarify the request from the information Rosa already has. He must not ask her to test or operate anything, expand the report to the whole office, or treat dead as proof of safe electrical status.
Language workshop: Which sockets, where, and since when?
Communication goal: Narrow a broad client description to named devices, location, and observation time without inferring technical status or requesting tests.
Language guidance: Dead is Rosa's informal description, not verified de-energized or isolated status. Her account identifies two desk receptacles in Room 204. Other areas are unassessed, so they must not be described as either working normally or affected.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I will record what you have already observed. / Which room does your report concern? / You mean the two desk receptacles in Room 204. / Is 08:30 when you first noticed the issue? / We have not established exactly when it began. / The client reports that those two receptacles are not working as expected. / Other areas have not been assessed. / I will not describe this as a verified office-wide outage. / The directory entry says office. / The directory's accuracy has not been verified. / Dead is your description, not verified electrical status. / I cannot infer that equipment is de-energized or safe from this report. / You do not need to test or operate anything for this clarification. / The report concerns two devices, not an unspecified number. / Two desk receptacles, Room 204, noticed at 08:30, other areas unassessed. / I will preserve the unverified directory entry separately from your device report.
Vocabulary: receptacle: Device providing contacts for a plug connection. / socket: Common term for a receptacle or plug-in connection point. / outlet: Point where electrical energy is supplied to utilization equipment. / desk receptacle: Receptacle serving or located at a desk position. / service request: Report asking for attention to a stated issue. / client account: Description supplied by the customer. / reported symptom: Problem described by a person rather than established by a technical finding. / observation time: When a person noticed the described condition. / onset: Time when a condition began, if established. / affected location: Area named in the report as experiencing the issue. / unassessed area: Location whose condition has not been evaluated. / panel directory: List identifying circuits or areas associated with a panel. / directory entry: Individual item in a panel or circuit list. / circuit identification: Establishing which circuit corresponds to a device or area. / circuit: Electrical path or connected arrangement through which current may flow. / distribution panel: Assembly distributing electrical supply to outgoing circuits. / IP code: Enclosure protection code with separate digits for solid-object and water protection. / room reference: Identifier locating a room in the building. / verified status: Condition established through the appropriate authorized process. / de-energized: In a state without energization, requiring appropriate verification before reliance. / isolation status: Established state of separation from relevant energy sources under the actual procedure. / technical finding: Conclusion supported by the relevant professional assessment. / scope of report: Extent of the issue actually described by the reporting person. / readback: Repetition of important information to confirm accuracy.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10, stressing two, Room 204, and noticed at 08:30. Switch roles for turns 11-20; keep dead attributed to the client, not a verified electrical state. Check and read the transfer without asking the client to operate or test anything.
Partner: Rosa: Client / Kai: Electrician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the intake readback from the client information. Do not turn the word dead or an unverified label into a technical conclusion.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rosa: Could you read back the room, count, and observation time? Those are the details I can confirm from what I already know.
Kai: The readback is: client reports two desk receptacles in Room 204, noticed at 08:30; exact onset unknown; other areas unassessed; directory wording unverified.
Rosa: Yes, that's accurate. Please retain office as unverified wording and keep it separate from the two devices I've reported.
Kai: I will. Circuit identification remains open for the appropriate process. The request will preserve your observations without asking you to carry out any electrical action or adding a technical finding.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Scope: The cases and products are fictional. This book teaches English, not electrical testing, switching, isolation, diagnosis, installation, circuit identification, or code compliance. Follow actual training, authorization, procedures, manufacturer information, and applicable requirements. Client descriptions, labels, outage plans, and handover records do not prove equipment is de-energized or safe to use or work on. These dialogues authorize no electrical operation, relabeling, routing change, substitution, or added load.
Lesson 01: Taking a precise electrical service request
Original fictional case: Client Rosa tells electrician Kai that the office sockets are dead. Her account concerns two desk receptacles in Room 204, noticed at 08:30. Other areas have not been assessed. A panel-directory entry says office, but its accuracy has not been verified. Kai must clarify the request from the information Rosa already has. He must not ask her to test or operate anything, expand the report to the whole office, or treat dead as proof of safe electrical status.
Language workshop: Which sockets, where, and since when?
Communication goal: Narrow a broad client description to named devices, location, and observation time without inferring technical status or requesting tests.
Language guidance: Dead is Rosa's informal description, not verified de-energized or isolated status. Her account identifies two desk receptacles in Room 204. Other areas are unassessed, so they must not be described as either working normally or affected.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I will record what you have already observed. / Which room does your report concern? / You mean the two desk receptacles in Room 204. / Is 08:30 when you first noticed the issue? / We have not established exactly when it began. / The client reports that those two receptacles are not working as expected. / Other areas have not been assessed. / I will not describe this as a verified office-wide outage. / The directory entry says office. / The directory's accuracy has not been verified. / Dead is your description, not verified electrical status. / I cannot infer that equipment is de-energized or safe from this report. / You do not need to test or operate anything for this clarification. / The report concerns two devices, not an unspecified number. / Two desk receptacles, Room 204, noticed at 08:30, other areas unassessed. / I will preserve the unverified directory entry separately from your device report.
Vocabulary: receptacle: Device providing contacts for a plug connection. / socket: Common term for a receptacle or plug-in connection point. / outlet: Point where electrical energy is supplied to utilization equipment. / desk receptacle: Receptacle serving or located at a desk position. / service request: Report asking for attention to a stated issue. / client account: Description supplied by the customer. / reported symptom: Problem described by a person rather than established by a technical finding. / observation time: When a person noticed the described condition. / onset: Time when a condition began, if established. / affected location: Area named in the report as experiencing the issue. / unassessed area: Location whose condition has not been evaluated. / panel directory: List identifying circuits or areas associated with a panel. / directory entry: Individual item in a panel or circuit list. / circuit identification: Establishing which circuit corresponds to a device or area. / circuit: Electrical path or connected arrangement through which current may flow. / distribution panel: Assembly distributing electrical supply to outgoing circuits. / IP code: Enclosure protection code with separate digits for solid-object and water protection. / room reference: Identifier locating a room in the building. / verified status: Condition established through the appropriate authorized process. / de-energized: In a state without energization, requiring appropriate verification before reliance. / isolation status: Established state of separation from relevant energy sources under the actual procedure. / technical finding: Conclusion supported by the relevant professional assessment. / scope of report: Extent of the issue actually described by the reporting person. / readback: Repetition of important information to confirm accuracy.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10, stressing two, Room 204, and noticed at 08:30. Switch roles for turns 11-20; keep dead attributed to the client, not a verified electrical state. Check and read the transfer without asking the client to operate or test anything.
Partner: Rosa: Client / Kai: Electrician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the intake readback from the client information. Do not turn the word dead or an unverified label into a technical conclusion.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rosa: Could you read back the room, count, and observation time? Those are the details I can confirm from what I already know.
Kai: The readback is: client reports two desk receptacles in Room 204, noticed at 08:30; exact onset unknown; other areas unassessed; directory wording unverified.
Rosa: Yes, that's accurate. Please retain office as unverified wording and keep it separate from the two devices I've reported.
Kai: I will. Circuit identification remains open for the appropriate process. The request will preserve your observations without asking you to carry out any electrical action or adding a technical finding.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

02Clarifying lighting specificationsMore kelvins does not mean more light+

Your goal

Explain the difference between light output and color temperature while preserving unresolved compatibility and substitution decisions.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

More kelvins does not mean more light

Client Ben has selected two fictional luminaires for comparison with electrician Asha. Both are listed at 1,200 lumens. One is 3,000 kelvins and the other 4,000 kelvins. Ben assumes the larger kelvin number means more light. No substitution is approved, and driver compatibility remains for specialist review. Asha must explain the two attributes without promising identical illumination at a work surface or treating equal lumen output as proof that the products are interchangeable.

Ben: Client / Asha: Electrician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What do both products list?

Answer and explanation

B. 1,200 lumens

Both luminaires list the same light output of 1,200 lumens.

2. What differs between them?

Answer and explanation

C. The 3,000 K and 4,000 K color-temperature values

The kelvin values differ, while the listed lumen values are equal.

3. What remains for specialist review?

Answer and explanation

A. Driver compatibility

Driver compatibility has not been established by the supplied product comparison.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Both luminaires are listed at 1,200 lumens, so the supplied light-output figures are equal. The 4,000 K product has a cooler color appearance than the 3,000 K product. The larger kelvin number does not establish a greater lumen output.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
luminaire
Complete lighting unit with its relevant light source and supporting components.compare the luminaires
lumen
Unit of luminous flux, describing visible light output.compare lumen output
luminous flux
Total visible light output weighted for human visual sensitivity.specify luminous flux
kelvin
Unit used to state correlated color temperature.read the kelvin value
correlated color temperature
Description of a light source's color appearance relative to a reference, abbreviated CCT.compare correlated color temperature
warm white
Description commonly used for a warmer white-light appearance.describe warm-white light
cool white
Description commonly used for a cooler white-light appearance.compare cool-white appearance
light output
Amount of light emitted, commonly stated in lumens.distinguish light output
illuminance
Amount of light arriving on a surface per unit area.assess illuminance at the work surface
lux
Unit of illuminance equal to one lumen per square meter.state illuminance in lux
light distribution
Pattern in which a luminaire sends light into a space.compare light distribution
beam angle
Angular spread used to describe a light beam under a stated convention.check the beam angle
LED
Light-emitting diode, a semiconductor light source.identify the LED product
driver
Device supplying and regulating electrical power for an LED system.review the driver specification
driver compatibility
Suitability of a driver for the specified lighting and control arrangement.verify driver compatibility
dimming
Adjustment of light output through a compatible control arrangement.review dimming requirements
control interface
Connection or protocol through which a lighting system is controlled.identify the control interface
watt
Unit of power, distinct from a measure of light output.compare input power in watts
efficacy
Light output per unit of input power, commonly lumens per watt.distinguish efficacy from color temperature
color rendering
Effect of a light source on the appearance of object colors.compare color rendering
color rendering index
Metric describing color fidelity relative to a reference, abbreviated CRI.check the color rendering index
product data sheet
Document describing specified product attributes.compare the product data sheets
substitution
Proposed replacement of a specified product with another.review a proposed substitution
specialist review
Assessment by a person with relevant technical competence.refer compatibility for specialist review

Equal listed lumens do not guarantee identical light levels at a desk, because distribution and installation conditions matter. No substitution is approved, and driver compatibility remains unresolved. Do not infer wattage, efficiency, dimming behavior, or technical interchangeability from these two numbers.

C Notice the language

Correct the comparison without making the client feel foolish

Lighting labels place several numbers close together, and a larger figure can seem like a simple improvement. Name what each unit describes, compare the supplied values, and keep separate questions such as distribution, compatibility, and approval visible.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the two attributes
Lumens and kelvins describe different things.
Explain output
Lumens describe the listed light output.
Explain color
Kelvins describe the color temperature.
Compare the given output
Both products are listed at 1,200 lumens.
Compare the color
The 4,000 K option has a cooler appearance than the 3,000 K option.
Correct the assumption
A larger kelvin number does not mean more lumen output.
Preserve the exact claim
Their listed outputs match; that is not a promise of identical desk illumination.
Separate distribution
We also need to consider how the light is distributed.
Avoid a wattage inference
The supplied figures do not tell us their input power.
Avoid an efficiency inference
We cannot rank efficiency from color temperature alone.
Keep compatibility open
Driver compatibility still needs specialist review.
Keep approval open
No product substitution is approved.
Distinguish comparison and replacement
Comparing them does not authorize swapping one for the other.
Refer the data
The specialist needs the relevant product and driver information.
Avoid a control promise
We have not established dimming compatibility.
Read back the result
Same listed lumens, different color temperature, and compatibility still unresolved.
6 language and precision notes
Listed at
Attributes the value to the supplied product information, not a site measurement.
Cooler
Describes white-light color appearance rather than physical temperature or greater output.
Same output
Refers to the stated lumens, not every lighting characteristic.
At the surface
Introduces illuminance, which is distinct from source output.
Compatible
Requires relevant technical assessment beyond matching two label numbers.
Approved substitution
A decision status that is separate from a comparison of specifications.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which correction is accurate?

Answer and explanation

D. Both list 1,200 lumens; 4,000 K describes a cooler color appearance, not more listed output.

The correction distinguishes the equal listed output from the differing color-temperature values.

2. Which claim is not supported by equal lumens?

Answer and explanation

B. Both will provide identical illumination at every desk.

Surface illumination also depends on distribution and installation conditions not supplied in the case.

3. What should happen with driver compatibility?

Answer and explanation

A. Refer it for specialist review using the relevant information

Matching light-output figures do not establish electrical or control compatibility.

4. What is the substitution status?

Answer and explanation

A. Not approved

The scenario explicitly states that no product substitution has been approved.

E Conversations

More kelvins does not mean more light

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ben: Client / Asha: Electrician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • correlated color temperature
  • Illuminance
  • specialist review
  • light distribution
  • substitution
  • watts
  • color appearance
  • lumen
  • product data sheet
  • Driver compatibility
  1. Ben
    These are the two fittings I'm comparing. The four-thousand-K version should give more light than the three-thousand-K one, shouldn't it?
  2. Asha
    That figure is the 1, not the output. Both products list twelve hundred lumens, so their stated outputs are equal.
  3. Ben
    I've been comparing the wrong numbers. Which figure should I use when I mean the amount of light coming from the fitting?
  4. Asha
    Use the 2 value. Both list twelve hundred here. Kelvins describe the white light's color appearance, not how much light it emits.
  5. Ben
    What would four thousand look like compared with three thousand? I'd like to describe the difference accurately when I speak to our designer.
  6. Asha
    It has a cooler 3. Cooler doesn't mean the fitting runs colder, and a higher kelvin figure doesn't mean more lumens.
  7. Ben
    If their outputs match, will they put the same light level on each desk? That's what matters most to the people using this room.
  8. Asha
    Not necessarily. 4 concerns the light arriving at the surface. The installation conditions and where the light goes both affect that result.
  9. Ben
    So the total leaving a fitting isn't the same as the amount arriving at one particular desk. We need more information for that comparison.
  10. Asha
    Yes, including the 5. Equal total outputs can be directed differently, so I won't promise identical light levels throughout the desk area.
  11. Ben
    What about electricity use? Does the higher color-temperature number mean more power, or is that another separate figure on the specification?
  12. Asha
    Power is stated in 6. Those values aren't supplied here, so neither the color difference nor the equal output establishes which uses more electricity.
  13. Ben
    Can the driver planned for the first fitting be used with the second? I don't want to discover a separate compatibility issue after choosing.
  14. Asha
    7 needs specialist review. Equal lumens don't establish that the electrical and control arrangements suit both products.
  15. Ben
    Please leave the order unchanged while we compare. I'm asking about the alternative, not giving permission to substitute it for the specified fitting.
  16. Asha
    Understood. No 8 is approved. I'll keep your comparison question separate from permission to change the order.
  17. Ben
    What should go to the specialist? The two label numbers clearly aren't enough to settle the driver and control questions.
  18. Asha
    Send the relevant 9 with the driver information. We haven't established dimming behavior or technical interchangeability in this conversation.
  19. Ben
    Then I can report equal listed output, different white-light appearance, and an unresolved replacement question. I shouldn't promise equal desk illumination.
  20. Asha
    Correct. The 10 remains outstanding. We can explain the labels now while leaving compatibility and substitution for the appropriate assessment.

Answers and explanations
  1. correlated color temperature Turn 2. Correlated color temperature describes color appearance, not the amount of light emitted by the luminaire.
  2. lumen Turn 4. Lumen is the unit for visible light output; both supplied product figures are 1,200 lumens.
  3. color appearance Turn 6. Color appearance is cooler at 4,000 K than at 3,000 K; this is separate from the equal listed lumen output.
  4. Illuminance Turn 8. Illuminance concerns light reaching a surface, which is not determined by the listed lumen output alone.
  5. light distribution Turn 10. Light distribution describes where emitted light goes and can affect surface illumination even when listed outputs match.
  6. watts Turn 12. Watts measure power, which is distinct from lumens and color temperature; no wattage comparison is supplied.
  7. Driver compatibility Turn 14. Driver compatibility remains for specialist review and cannot be inferred from matching lumen values or a color-temperature comparison.
  8. substitution Turn 16. Substitution is not approved; discussing the alternative does not authorize replacing a specified product.
  9. product data sheet Turn 18. Product data sheet provides product-specific information for review; a brief comparison does not establish all compatibility requirements.
  10. specialist review Turn 20. Specialist review remains the next step for compatibility; neither equal output nor a cooler appearance resolves the substitution decision.
Read the complete conversation

Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Kelvins are not lumens

An electrician compares the client's two proposed luminaires.

  1. Electrician: Both luminaires are listed at twelve hundred lumens. Their color temperatures are different.
  2. Client: I thought the four-thousand-kelvin one would give us more light than the three-thousand one.
  3. Electrician: The kelvin figure describes warm or cool appearance, not the amount of light emitted.
  4. Client: So the four-thousand option looks cooler, while their stated output is the same?
  5. Electrician: Yes. That's the comparison these figures support, without settling the room's lighting design.
  6. Client: Will equal lumens guarantee that the desk looks equally bright under either fitting?
  7. Electrician: No, the fitting's distribution and the room arrangement also matter to that assessment.
  8. Client: Then I'd like the designer to compare their appearance and suitability for this desk.
  9. Electrician: I'll include both data sheets and keep driver compatibility as a separate review point.
  10. Client: Good. My preference is cooler light, but that isn't approval of an unchecked substitution.
The fitting code has a suffix

An electrician checks a supplier's proposed lighting delivery.

  1. Electrician: The delivery note lists Lumenia forty B, while our approved schedule says forty D.
  2. Supplier: The main model name matches. Does the final letter matter on this order?
  3. Electrician: It may identify a different variant. Please compare the complete codes, not just the name.
  4. Supplier: I can ask the manufacturer what each suffix means and send the matching data sheets.
  5. Electrician: Please include the driver information too; the visible housing isn't the whole specification.
  6. Supplier: Understood. Should I dispatch B now and leave the paperwork review until it arrives?
  7. Electrician: No substitution is agreed. Keep the order pending while we resolve the variant.
  8. Supplier: I'll retain the original requirement and label B as a proposed alternative only.
  9. Electrician: Thank you. A clear comparison will help the reviewer decide without guessing from product photographs.
  10. Supplier: I'll return with the full-code comparison and a separate availability update for the specified version.
Dimmable does not identify the control

An electrician explains a product-description limit to an interior designer.

  1. Electrician: This catalogue calls the luminaire dimmable, but it doesn't identify the proposed control arrangement.
  2. Interior designer: The client thought dimmable meant it would work with any control already in the room.
  3. Electrician: That description alone doesn't confirm compatibility between the light source, driver, and control.
  4. Interior designer: Could we ask the supplier for confirmation against the actual equipment references in our schedule?
  5. Electrician: Yes, please include all those references rather than only the luminaire's marketing name.
  6. Interior designer: I'll also ask whether the proposed alternate uses the same driver as the approved fitting.
  7. Electrician: Good. We should compare documented combinations, not infer compatibility from the same appearance.
  8. Interior designer: The client wants a simple explanation. How should I describe why selection remains pending?
  9. Electrician: Say the light can be dimmed in a suitable arrangement, but this combination is unconfirmed.
  10. Interior designer: I'll use that wording and keep the product preference separate from technical acceptance.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

More kelvins does not mean more light
  1. Read turns 1-10; distinguish lumens, kelvins, and light arriving at the desk.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress watts, compatibility, and substitution as separate questions.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Keep light output and color separate

Complete the client explanation using the supplied values. Do not infer a different output, identical desk illumination, or approved compatibility.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Electrician: "Both products list 1,200 ___."

Answer and explanation

D. lumens

Lumens describe the equal light-output figures supplied for the two products.

2. Client: "The 3,000 and 4,000 values are in ___."

Answer and explanation

A. kelvins

Kelvins identify the color-temperature values rather than the output figures.

3. Electrician: "The 4,000 K option has a ___ appearance."

Answer and explanation

B. cooler

Cooler compares the white-light appearance with that of the 3,000 K option.

4. Client: "Driver compatibility still needs specialist ___."

Answer and explanation

A. review

Compatibility remains unresolved and requires the stated specialist review.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 02: Clarifying lighting specifications
Original fictional case: Client Ben has selected two fictional luminaires for comparison with electrician Asha. Both are listed at 1,200 lumens. One is 3,000 kelvins and the other 4,000 kelvins. Ben assumes the larger kelvin number means more light. No substitution is approved, and driver compatibility remains for specialist review. Asha must explain the two attributes without promising identical illumination at a work surface or treating equal lumen output as proof that the products are interchangeable.
Language workshop: More kelvins does not mean more light
Communication goal: Explain the difference between light output and color temperature while preserving unresolved compatibility and substitution decisions.
Language guidance: Both luminaires are listed at 1,200 lumens, so the supplied light-output figures are equal. The 4,000 K product has a cooler color appearance than the 3,000 K product. The larger kelvin number does not establish a greater lumen output.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Lumens and kelvins describe different things. / Lumens describe the listed light output. / Kelvins describe the color temperature. / Both products are listed at 1,200 lumens. / The 4,000 K option has a cooler appearance than the 3,000 K option. / A larger kelvin number does not mean more lumen output. / Their listed outputs match; that is not a promise of identical desk illumination. / We also need to consider how the light is distributed. / The supplied figures do not tell us their input power. / We cannot rank efficiency from color temperature alone. / Driver compatibility still needs specialist review. / No product substitution is approved. / Comparing them does not authorize swapping one for the other. / The specialist needs the relevant product and driver information. / We have not established dimming compatibility. / Same listed lumens, different color temperature, and compatibility still unresolved.
Vocabulary: luminaire: Complete lighting unit with its relevant light source and supporting components. / lumen: Unit of luminous flux, describing visible light output. / luminous flux: Total visible light output weighted for human visual sensitivity. / kelvin: Unit used to state correlated color temperature. / correlated color temperature: Description of a light source's color appearance relative to a reference, abbreviated CCT. / warm white: Description commonly used for a warmer white-light appearance. / cool white: Description commonly used for a cooler white-light appearance. / light output: Amount of light emitted, commonly stated in lumens. / illuminance: Amount of light arriving on a surface per unit area. / lux: Unit of illuminance equal to one lumen per square meter. / light distribution: Pattern in which a luminaire sends light into a space. / beam angle: Angular spread used to describe a light beam under a stated convention. / LED: Light-emitting diode, a semiconductor light source. / driver: Device supplying and regulating electrical power for an LED system. / driver compatibility: Suitability of a driver for the specified lighting and control arrangement. / dimming: Adjustment of light output through a compatible control arrangement. / control interface: Connection or protocol through which a lighting system is controlled. / watt: Unit of power, distinct from a measure of light output. / efficacy: Light output per unit of input power, commonly lumens per watt. / color rendering: Effect of a light source on the appearance of object colors. / color rendering index: Metric describing color fidelity relative to a reference, abbreviated CRI. / product data sheet: Document describing specified product attributes. / substitution: Proposed replacement of a specified product with another. / specialist review: Assessment by a person with relevant technical competence.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; distinguish lumens, kelvins, and light arriving at the desk. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress watts, compatibility, and substitution as separate questions. Check and read the transfer, keeping cooler separate from brighter or lower power use.
Partner: Ben: Client / Asha: Electrician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the client explanation using the supplied values. Do not infer a different output, identical desk illumination, or approved compatibility.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ben: What should go to the specialist? The two label numbers clearly aren't enough to settle the driver and control questions.
Asha: Send the relevant product data sheet with the driver information. We haven't established dimming behavior or technical interchangeability in this conversation.
Ben: Then I can report equal listed output, different white-light appearance, and an unresolved replacement question. I shouldn't promise equal desk illumination.
Asha: Correct. The specialist review remains outstanding. We can explain the labels now while leaving compatibility and substitution for the appropriate assessment.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

03Agreeing device positions and drawing referencesCenterline or lower edge?+

Your goal

Clarify the datum and device reference behind a mounting dimension without treating another trade drawing as installation approval.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

Centerline or lower edge?

Electrician Leila and joinery coordinator Omar review a reception elevation showing a device at 1,100 millimeters above finished floor level. The electrical note does not identify whether the figure refers to the device centerline or lower edge. The joinery drawing uses a centerline. No position has been approved for installation. Leila needs the responsible designer to clarify the intended reference rather than silently import the joinery convention into the electrical detail.

Omar: Joinery coordinator / Leila: Electrician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is stated on the reception elevation?

Answer and explanation

D. 1,100 millimeters above finished floor level, with the device reference unclear

The elevation states the height and floor datum but not the device endpoint.

2. What convention does the joinery drawing use?

Answer and explanation

D. Centerline

The joinery drawing uses a centerline, but that does not resolve the electrical note.

3. What is the installation status?

Answer and explanation

A. No device position is approved

The case explicitly leaves installation position unapproved pending clarification.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The floor datum is stated: finished floor level. The missing information is the endpoint on the device, centerline or lower edge. The joinery centerline convention highlights the need to coordinate, but does not authorize applying that convention to the electrical note.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
finished floor level
Level of the completed floor surface, commonly abbreviated FFL.confirm finished floor level
above finished floor
Dimension measured upward from the completed floor, commonly abbreviated AFF.state the height above finished floor
centerline
Reference line through the specified center of a component.identify the centerline
lower edge
Bottom boundary of the specified device or component.locate the lower edge
upper edge
Top boundary of the specified device or component.distinguish the upper edge
mounting height
Specified height for locating a mounted device.clarify the mounting height
device position
Intended location of a specified component.confirm the device position
reception elevation
Drawing view showing the reception face and its details.review the reception elevation
datum level
Reference level from which a height is measured.verify the datum level
dimension endpoint
Point or edge to which a dimension extends.identify the dimension endpoint
set-out
Defined positioning information for locating work.clarify the set-out information
joinery interface
Point where electrical work and fitted woodwork must coordinate.review the joinery interface
drawing convention
Agreed or customary way information is represented on a drawing.clarify the drawing convention
reference ambiguity
Uncertainty about what a dimension or note refers to.flag the reference ambiguity
faceplate
Visible plate associated with an electrical accessory or device.identify the faceplate
back box
Enclosure behind a device or accessory.distinguish the back box
device schedule
List identifying specified devices and their attributes.check the device schedule
horizontal alignment
Relative positioning along a horizontal direction.coordinate horizontal alignment
vertical alignment
Relative positioning along a vertical direction.confirm vertical alignment
approved location
Position explicitly accepted through the relevant process.verify the approved location
design clarification
Explanation resolving uncertain design information.request design clarification
coordination mark-up
Annotated drawing used to communicate coordination questions.prepare a coordination mark-up
installation instruction
Authorized direction for fitting the specified equipment.distinguish an installation instruction
reference readback
Repetition of the datum and endpoint to verify shared understanding.give a reference readback

No device dimensions are supplied, so no centerline-to-edge conversion can be calculated here. No position is approved for installation. The next step is design clarification of the intended reference, with both drawings included in the coordination question.

C Notice the language

Shared numbers still need shared reference points

Two trades may use the same dimension while measuring to different parts of a component. Name the datum and endpoint separately. Another drawing can reveal a question to resolve, but its convention does not automatically answer an unclear note in a different discipline.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Read the stated height
The elevation shows 1,100 millimeters above finished floor level.
Identify the known datum
The reference level is the completed floor surface.
Identify the missing endpoint
The note does not say centerline or lower edge.
Cite the other drawing
The joinery drawing uses a centerline.
Avoid importing a convention
That does not automatically settle the electrical reference.
Ask a precise question
Does 1,100 refer to the device centerline or its lower edge?
Keep the datum unchanged
I am not asking to change the finished-floor reference.
Keep the value unchanged
The query concerns the endpoint, not a new height.
Avoid a guessed conversion
We do not have the device dimensions needed for a conversion.
Preserve approval status
No installation position has been approved.
Request coordinated review
Please review the reception elevation with the joinery drawing.
Distinguish a mark-up from approval
A coordination note is not an installation instruction.
Name the interface
This is a device-position question at the joinery interface.
Ask for explicit clarification
The reply should state the datum and the device reference.
Read back the known facts
1,100 above finished floor, endpoint unclear, joinery centerline shown.
Close without setting a position
We need clarification before presenting either interpretation as approved.
6 language and precision notes
Above finished floor
Defines the vertical origin of the measurement, not its endpoint on the device.
Centerline versus edge
Different references that can produce different positions from the same stated number.
Uses
Describes the other drawing's convention without transferring its authority.
Clarify versus change
Resolving meaning does not necessarily request a different design.
Shown versus approved
A displayed dimension and an accepted installation position are distinct statuses.
Both drawings
Keeps the interface visible for coordinated interpretation.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which question addresses the ambiguity?

Answer and explanation

A. Is the 1,100-millimeter height to the centerline or lower edge of the device?

The question targets the missing endpoint while preserving the stated height and floor datum.

2. Which fact is already known?

Answer and explanation

A. The datum is finished floor level

The elevation names finished floor level even though the device reference remains unclear.

3. Which inference is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. The joinery centerline automatically approves the electrical centerline position.

A convention on another drawing does not resolve or authorize the ambiguous electrical detail.

4. What should the clarification preserve?

Answer and explanation

A. Both drawing references, the stated height, the floor datum, and the unanswered endpoint

These details define the actual coordination question without adding dimensions or permission.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

Centerline or lower edge?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Omar: Joinery coordinator / Leila: Electrician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • approved location
  • joinery interface
  • reference ambiguity
  • mounting height
  • Finished floor level
