  1. Omar
    Our joinery drawing uses a centerline at eleven hundred above finished floor. Can the electrical position use that reference, or does your note leave it open?
  2. Leila
    Not yet. The 1 is shown, but the electrical note does not say whether it refers to the centerline or the lower edge.
  3. Omar
    So finished floor tells us where the dimension starts. The uncertainty is where it ends on the device, not which floor level to use.
  4. Leila
    Yes. 2 is the stated datum. The missing part is the reference on the device, and we should keep those two questions separate.
  5. Omar
    I'll attach the joinery detail to explain why I expected a centerline. It shouldn't be taken as an answer for the electrical designer.
  6. Leila
    That will help explain the 3. The other drawing is relevant evidence for the question, not permission to fill in the missing electrical reference ourselves.
  7. Omar
    Can the reply name the feature explicitly? Repeating eleven hundred won't resolve whether it's centerline, lower edge, faceplate, or another reference.
  8. Leila
    We need an explicit 4. The reply should identify the intended device reference, including whether the note means centerline or lower edge.
  9. Omar
    Can we calculate the difference between those positions, or is the device size missing as well as the intended endpoint?
  10. Leila
    We do not have the device dimensions for that calculation. A 5 is not solved by inventing an offset or assuming a particular faceplate size.
  11. Omar
    Then I won't sketch a guessed offset. We're asking what the existing number means, not proposing a different height.
  12. Leila
    Exactly. The 6 concerns what the existing note means. We are not asking to change the 1,100-millimeter value or the finished-floor datum without a design decision.
  13. Omar
    No position is approved for installation yet, correct? That must be clear before the query reaches the site team.
  14. Leila
    Correct. There is no 7 from this conversation. Neither interpretation should be presented as an authorized installation position while the reference remains unresolved.
  15. Omar
    I can put both views on a coordination mark-up. How do I avoid making it look like I've approved the centerline?
  16. Leila
    A 8 is useful for the review. It should identify the uncertainty clearly, not look like an instruction to install at the position you have assumed.
  17. Omar
    Give me the precise query, please. The responsible designer needs to answer the reference question, not comment generally on reception.
  18. Leila
    Use this 9: 1,100 millimeters above finished floor level; electrical endpoint unclear; joinery uses centerline; please confirm the intended device reference with both drawings reviewed together.
  19. Omar
    I'll keep the value, floor datum, and unresolved endpoint together. The comparison stays a question until the proper clarification arrives.
  20. Leila
    Thank you. The clarification is not an 10 until the relevant process establishes what is intended and approved. For now, the endpoint remains open and no position is authorized here.

Answers and explanations
  1. mounting height Turn 2. Mounting height is stated as 1,100 millimeters, but the point on the device to which it applies remains unclear.
  2. Finished floor level Turn 4. Finished floor level is the stated datum; the ambiguity concerns the device endpoint rather than the floor reference.
  3. joinery interface Turn 6. Joinery interface identifies the coordination point between the device and fitted woodwork without authorizing a position.
  4. dimension endpoint Turn 8. Dimension endpoint is the particular point or edge being measured to, which must be clarified instead of merely repeating 1,100.
  5. reference ambiguity Turn 10. Reference ambiguity cannot be resolved by a guessed conversion when the device dimensions and intended endpoint are not established.
  6. design clarification Turn 12. Design clarification asks what the current note means; it does not automatically request a new height or authorize a position.
  7. approved location Turn 14. Approved location has not been established, so neither centerline nor lower-edge interpretation can be presented as ready for installation.
  8. coordination mark-up Turn 16. Coordination mark-up communicates the unresolved interface question and is not itself an instruction or approval to install.
  9. reference readback Turn 18. Reference readback preserves the 1,100-millimeter height, finished-floor datum, and unresolved device endpoint together.
  10. installation instruction Turn 20. Installation instruction is not supplied by the unresolved query; the team still needs the intended reference and appropriate approval.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Omar: Our joinery drawing uses a centerline at eleven hundred above finished floor. Can the electrical position use that reference, or does your note leave it open?
  2. Leila: Not yet. The mounting height is shown, but the electrical note does not say whether it refers to the centerline or the lower edge.
  3. Omar: So finished floor tells us where the dimension starts. The uncertainty is where it ends on the device, not which floor level to use.
  4. Leila: Yes. Finished floor level is the stated datum. The missing part is the reference on the device, and we should keep those two questions separate.
  5. Omar: I'll attach the joinery detail to explain why I expected a centerline. It shouldn't be taken as an answer for the electrical designer.
  6. Leila: That will help explain the joinery interface. The other drawing is relevant evidence for the question, not permission to fill in the missing electrical reference ourselves.
  7. Omar: Can the reply name the feature explicitly? Repeating eleven hundred won't resolve whether it's centerline, lower edge, faceplate, or another reference.
  8. Leila: We need an explicit dimension endpoint. The reply should identify the intended device reference, including whether the note means centerline or lower edge.
  9. Omar: Can we calculate the difference between those positions, or is the device size missing as well as the intended endpoint?
  10. Leila: We do not have the device dimensions for that calculation. A reference ambiguity is not solved by inventing an offset or assuming a particular faceplate size.
  11. Omar: Then I won't sketch a guessed offset. We're asking what the existing number means, not proposing a different height.
  12. Leila: Exactly. The design clarification concerns what the existing note means. We are not asking to change the 1,100-millimeter value or the finished-floor datum without a design decision.
  13. Omar: No position is approved for installation yet, correct? That must be clear before the query reaches the site team.
  14. Leila: Correct. There is no approved location from this conversation. Neither interpretation should be presented as an authorized installation position while the reference remains unresolved.
  15. Omar: I can put both views on a coordination mark-up. How do I avoid making it look like I've approved the centerline?
  16. Leila: A coordination mark-up is useful for the review. It should identify the uncertainty clearly, not look like an instruction to install at the position you have assumed.
  17. Omar: Give me the precise query, please. The responsible designer needs to answer the reference question, not comment generally on reception.
  18. Leila: Use this reference readback: 1,100 millimeters above finished floor level; electrical endpoint unclear; joinery uses centerline; please confirm the intended device reference with both drawings reviewed together.
  19. Omar: I'll keep the value, floor datum, and unresolved endpoint together. The comparison stays a question until the proper clarification arrives.
  20. Leila: Thank you. The clarification is not an installation instruction until the relevant process establishes what is intended and approved. For now, the endpoint remains open and no position is authorized here.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Eleven hundred to which point?

An electrician and designer resolve the question to be answered for reception.

  1. Electrician: The mounting height is eleven hundred millimeters, but which device point is measured?
  2. Designer: Is the uncertainty about the floor level or about the device itself?
  3. Electrician: The device point. Finished floor level is named, but centerline or lower edge isn't.
  4. Designer: The joinery detail uses the centerline. I need to reconcile the electrical note with that.
  5. Electrician: Thank you. The client assumed identical numbers guaranteed the two components would align.
  6. Designer: They don't if the reference points differ. I'll issue a coordinated note for that location.
  7. Electrician: Should I keep the current position marked as proposed until the clarification comes back?
  8. Designer: Yes. Don't describe it as an agreed installation location while the point remains unresolved.
  9. Electrician: I'll explain the difference to the client and identify both drawing references in the query.
  10. Designer: That will let us answer the actual positioning question instead of debating the number alone.
The ceiling plan and the table move

An electrician and project coordinator discuss a revised meeting-room layout.

  1. Electrician: The furniture plan moved the meeting table, but the reflected ceiling plan hasn't changed.
  2. Project coordinator: The client wants the pendant centered above the table in the new layout.
  3. Electrician: Then we need a coordinated lighting position rather than assuming the old centerline still applies.
  4. Project coordinator: Could you tell the crew to follow the table when the furniture arrives?
  5. Electrician: The table's final position isn't a documented installation reference. Let's ask the designer for dimensions.
  6. Project coordinator: I can send both plans and identify which furniture revision the client has selected.
  7. Electrician: Please include the pendant tag so the response refers to this fitting specifically.
  8. Project coordinator: The client also moved the credenza. Should that be part of the same coordination review?
  9. Electrician: Yes, flag it as a separate changed element, not an assumed instruction about nearby devices.
  10. Project coordinator: I'll request a revised coordinated layout and keep both affected positions pending that response.
Left as viewed from where?

An electrician clarifies a client's description of a proposed wall control position.

  1. Electrician: You requested the control on the left of the doorway. From which viewpoint?
  2. Client: I meant standing inside the room and looking toward the corridor through the doorway.
  3. Electrician: Thanks. The supplier's sketch looks from the corridor, so left refers to the opposite side.
  4. Client: That explains why their marked position looked wrong when I reviewed it yesterday.
  5. Electrician: Could we identify the wall and dimension reference instead of relying on left alone?
  6. Client: Yes, the wall beside the storage cupboard is the side I actually mean.
  7. Electrician: I'll mark that proposed location on the room elevation for the designer's review.
  8. Client: Please label the viewing direction as well, because the plan is hard for me to visualize.
  9. Electrician: I will, and the final position will remain subject to the coordinated design confirmation.
  10. Client: Good. My preference is now clear without either of us guessing from a mirrored viewpoint.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Centerline or lower edge?
  1. Read turns 1-10, stressing the known floor datum and unknown device endpoint.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep the joinery convention separate from electrical approval.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Name both ends of the dimension

Complete the coordination query. Preserve the height, known floor datum, unclear endpoint, and lack of installation approval.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Electrician: "The stated height is ___ millimeters."

Answer and explanation

B. 1,100

The numerical height remains 1,100; the query does not replace it.

2. Coordinator: "It is measured above finished ___ level."

Answer and explanation

A. floor

Finished floor level is the stated datum for the dimension.

3. Electrician: "The device reference could be centerline or lower ___."

Answer and explanation

C. edge

Lower edge is the alternative endpoint that the note leaves unclear.

4. Coordinator: "No installation position is ___ yet."

Answer and explanation

D. approved

The position remains unapproved until the relevant clarification and approval process.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 03: Agreeing device positions and drawing references
Original fictional case: Electrician Leila and joinery coordinator Omar review a reception elevation showing a device at 1,100 millimeters above finished floor level. The electrical note does not identify whether the figure refers to the device centerline or lower edge. The joinery drawing uses a centerline. No position has been approved for installation. Leila needs the responsible designer to clarify the intended reference rather than silently import the joinery convention into the electrical detail.
Language workshop: Centerline or lower edge?
Communication goal: Clarify the datum and device reference behind a mounting dimension without treating another trade drawing as installation approval.
Language guidance: The floor datum is stated: finished floor level. The missing information is the endpoint on the device, centerline or lower edge. The joinery centerline convention highlights the need to coordinate, but does not authorize applying that convention to the electrical note.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The elevation shows 1,100 millimeters above finished floor level. / The reference level is the completed floor surface. / The note does not say centerline or lower edge. / The joinery drawing uses a centerline. / That does not automatically settle the electrical reference. / Does 1,100 refer to the device centerline or its lower edge? / I am not asking to change the finished-floor reference. / The query concerns the endpoint, not a new height. / We do not have the device dimensions needed for a conversion. / No installation position has been approved. / Please review the reception elevation with the joinery drawing. / A coordination note is not an installation instruction. / This is a device-position question at the joinery interface. / The reply should state the datum and the device reference. / 1,100 above finished floor, endpoint unclear, joinery centerline shown. / We need clarification before presenting either interpretation as approved.
Vocabulary: finished floor level: Level of the completed floor surface, commonly abbreviated FFL. / above finished floor: Dimension measured upward from the completed floor, commonly abbreviated AFF. / centerline: Reference line through the specified center of a component. / lower edge: Bottom boundary of the specified device or component. / upper edge: Top boundary of the specified device or component. / mounting height: Specified height for locating a mounted device. / device position: Intended location of a specified component. / reception elevation: Drawing view showing the reception face and its details. / datum level: Reference level from which a height is measured. / dimension endpoint: Point or edge to which a dimension extends. / set-out: Defined positioning information for locating work. / joinery interface: Point where electrical work and fitted woodwork must coordinate. / drawing convention: Agreed or customary way information is represented on a drawing. / reference ambiguity: Uncertainty about what a dimension or note refers to. / faceplate: Visible plate associated with an electrical accessory or device. / back box: Enclosure behind a device or accessory. / device schedule: List identifying specified devices and their attributes. / horizontal alignment: Relative positioning along a horizontal direction. / vertical alignment: Relative positioning along a vertical direction. / approved location: Position explicitly accepted through the relevant process. / design clarification: Explanation resolving uncertain design information. / coordination mark-up: Annotated drawing used to communicate coordination questions. / installation instruction: Authorized direction for fitting the specified equipment. / reference readback: Repetition of the datum and endpoint to verify shared understanding.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10, stressing the known floor datum and unknown device endpoint. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep the joinery convention separate from electrical approval. Check the transfer, then read the query with 1,100 unchanged and no guessed conversion.
Partner: Omar: Joinery coordinator / Leila: Electrician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the coordination query. Preserve the height, known floor datum, unclear endpoint, and lack of installation approval.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Omar: Give me the precise query, please. The responsible designer needs to answer the reference question, not comment generally on reception.
Leila: Use this reference readback: 1,100 millimeters above finished floor level; electrical endpoint unclear; joinery uses centerline; please confirm the intended device reference with both drawings reviewed together.
Omar: I'll keep the value, floor datum, and unresolved endpoint together. The comparison stays a question until the proper clarification arrives.
Leila: Thank you. The clarification is not an installation instruction until the relevant process establishes what is intended and approved. For now, the endpoint remains open and no position is authorized here.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Coordinating containment and shared work areasA tray and a duct in bay C3+

Your goal

Describe a cross-trade drawing conflict, request joint review, and preserve the distinction between a meeting and an approved route.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

A tray and a duct in bay C3

Electrical drawing E6 places a cable tray in ceiling bay C3. The mechanical layout shows a duct in the same space. Electrician Dev reports the conflict to ceiling coordinator Elena before the coordination meeting at 13:00. Neither trade has agreed a revised route. Dev needs both drawings reviewed together. He must not announce an invented offset, assume one trade has priority, or treat the meeting time as authorization to install a changed route.

Elena: Ceiling coordinator / Dev: Electrician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What occupies the same drawn space?

Answer and explanation

A. A cable tray on E6 and a duct on the mechanical layout in bay C3

The electrical tray and mechanical duct conflict in the same ceiling bay.

2. What is scheduled for 13:00?

Answer and explanation

D. The ceiling coordination meeting

The supplied time is the meeting time, not approval or completion.

3. What is the revised-route status?

Answer and explanation

B. Neither trade has agreed one

No revised route is agreed by either trade in the supplied facts.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

E6 places a cable tray in ceiling bay C3 where the mechanical layout shows a duct. The conflict is between the drawn arrangements; the case does not establish completed installation. No revised route or priority rule is agreed.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
cable tray
Support system used to carry cables along a route.identify the cable tray
containment
Systems supporting or enclosing cables along their route.coordinate cable containment
conduit
Tube or pipe used as an electrical raceway.distinguish conduit from cable tray
trunking
Enclosed channel used to contain cables in relevant terminology.identify the trunking route
raceway
Channel designed to contain electrical conductors or cables.specify the raceway
duct
Passage or enclosure conveying air in a mechanical system.locate the mechanical duct
ceiling bay
Defined section of ceiling space used as a location reference.identify ceiling bay C3
ceiling void
Space above a ceiling surface.coordinate work in the ceiling void
mechanical layout
Drawing showing the arrangement of mechanical services.compare the mechanical layout
electrical layout
Drawing showing the arrangement of electrical work.review the electrical layout
route conflict
Incompatibility between proposed paths or occupied spaces.flag a route conflict
spatial clash
Overlap or incompatible use of physical space.report a spatial clash
clearance requirement
Space needed around a component for the relevant purpose.verify clearance requirements
maintenance access
Space or route needed for inspection or servicing.preserve maintenance access
support arrangement
Specified way a component is held in place.review the support arrangement
coordinated drawing
Drawing incorporating agreed interfaces among relevant disciplines.issue a coordinated drawing
revised route
Changed path proposed or agreed for a service.review a revised route
proposed offset
Suggested displacement from a stated route or position.assess a proposed offset
trade priority
Claimed or agreed precedence between work disciplines.avoid assuming trade priority
joint review
Assessment involving the relevant parties together.request a joint review
coordination meeting
Discussion intended to resolve interfaces and arrangements.attend the coordination meeting
route approval
Acceptance of a specified routing arrangement.confirm route approval
drawing overlay
Combined view of related drawings for comparison.use a drawing overlay
unresolved interface
Cross-discipline connection or conflict not yet settled.record an unresolved interface

The meeting at 13:00 is a review point, not a route approval or installation start. Both drawings need joint review. No alternative route, offset, clearance, support detail, or maintenance-access solution is supplied for students to invent.

C Notice the language

Describe the interface without turning it into a contest

A shared-space conflict needs a coordinated answer, not a claim that one trade must move because the other spoke first. Name the components, location, and drawing sources, then state the unresolved route and the agreed review point.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Locate the conflict
The clash is in ceiling bay C3.
Cite the electrical source
E6 shows the cable tray in that space.
Cite the mechanical source
The mechanical layout shows a duct there.
State the interface
The two drawn routes occupy the same space.
Request joint review
We need both drawings reviewed together.
Preserve the current status
Neither trade has agreed a revised route.
Avoid priority assumptions
I am not assuming that one trade automatically takes priority.
Avoid an invented offset
No offset has been proposed and approved here.
State the meeting time
The ceiling coordination meeting is at 13:00.
Separate meeting and approval
The meeting time does not authorize a routing change.
Keep related requirements open
Clearances and access would need review for any proposed solution.
Avoid an installation claim
I am describing the drawings, not a completed site installation.
Keep the record specific
Cable tray and duct conflict at C3, with route revision unresolved.
Ask for an explicit outcome
The review needs to identify the agreed arrangement through the project process.
Preserve both sources
Please keep E6 and the mechanical layout attached to the query.
Close with the open issue
The interface remains unresolved pending coordinated review.
6 language and precision notes
Shows
Attributes the position to a drawing rather than claiming it is installed.
Same space
Defines the conflict between the two arrangements.
Neither
Makes clear that no trade has agreed the revised route.
Together
Calls for coordinated review rather than separate assumptions.
At 13:00
Identifies the meeting, not the time a technical solution becomes approved.
Proposed versus agreed
A possible change and an accepted route are different stages.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which report identifies the conflict precisely?

Answer and explanation

D. E6 cable tray and mechanical-layout duct share ceiling bay C3; revised route unresolved.

The report names both components, sources, location, and unresolved route status.

2. What does Dev need at the meeting?

Answer and explanation

A. Both drawings reviewed together

Joint review is needed to resolve the cross-trade conflict without unilateral assumptions.

3. Which statement overstates the 13:00 arrangement?

Answer and explanation

B. The new route is approved for installation at 13:00.

The meeting time establishes no route approval or installation authorization.

4. What must remain open for a proposed solution?

Answer and explanation

B. The actual route and relevant technical coordination requirements

No alternative route or supporting technical details have been established in the case.

E Conversations

A tray and a duct in bay C3

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Elena: Ceiling coordinator / Dev: Electrician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • electrical layout
  • ceiling bay
  • revised route
  • route conflict
  • trade priority
  • unresolved interface
  • Maintenance access
  • joint review
  • coordination meeting
  • route approval
  1. Elena
    Dev, what is the ceiling issue for thirteen hundred? Give me the bay and references so I can put the right drawings on the agenda.
  2. Dev
    There's a 1 at C3. E6 shows a cable tray in the same space where the mechanical layout shows a duct.
  3. Elena
    Is that what the drawings show, or are you reporting an installed clash? Those need different descriptions in the coordination record.
  4. Dev
    I'm comparing the 2 against the mechanical layout. The case doesn't establish an installed clash or authorize any physical change.
  5. Elena
    Has either team agreed another path in a separate discussion? I'd rather check that before reopening something already settled.
  6. Dev
    Neither has agreed a 3. We have the conflict and the two sources, but no accepted alternative to present.
  7. Elena
    Then we need both drawings in the meeting, not just an electrical screenshot that leaves out the mechanical constraint.
  8. Dev
    A 4 is needed. Either drawing alone would omit the other service and conceal the interface we need to resolve.
  9. Elena
    Is there a project decision giving one service precedence here? I don't want competing assumptions that the other trade must move.
  10. Dev
    No 5 is established. I won't assign the move to mechanical or promise an electrical change before the coordinated review.
  11. Elena
    Could the mark-up highlight the conflict without adding a new route? A suggested line can easily be forwarded as an accepted solution.
  12. Dev
    I'll identify 6 C3 and preserve both shown positions. No offset is agreed, so the query shouldn't show an invented change as approved.
  13. Elena
    If a new path is proposed, the review still needs clearances, supports, and access. Shifting a line doesn't settle all those requirements.
  14. Dev
    Correct. 7 is one of those questions. This issue note doesn't provide a complete alternative arrangement for the team to install.
  15. Elena
    Please label thirteen hundred explicitly as the meeting time. It isn't a guaranteed resolution time or an installation start.
  16. Dev
    I'll call it the 8. It tells people when to review the information, not when a changed route becomes authorized.
  17. Elena
    Read the short agenda entry back. I need both services, the bay, and the outstanding decision together.
  18. Dev
    E6 tray and mechanical-layout duct share C3; 9 remains outstanding; both drawings need review at thirteen hundred. Neither trade has accepted a revision.
  19. Elena
    That gives the meeting a precise issue without assigning blame. I'll retain both sources with it.
  20. Dev
    I'll keep it as an 10 until the project process records an agreed arrangement. The meeting and query don't themselves authorize a routing change.

Answers and explanations
  1. route conflict Turn 2. Route conflict is the cable tray and duct occupying the same drawn space in ceiling bay C3.
  2. electrical layout Turn 4. Electrical layout E6 shows the proposed tray position; the case does not establish that either service is already installed.
  3. revised route Turn 6. Revised route remains unagreed by both trades, so no changed path can be presented as a settled arrangement.
  4. joint review Turn 8. Joint review brings both drawing arrangements into the same discussion instead of assuming either trade can resolve the interface alone.
  5. trade priority Turn 10. Trade priority is not established in the case, so neither electrical nor mechanical can be assumed to take precedence automatically.
  6. ceiling bay Turn 12. Ceiling bay C3 is the precise shared-space reference, not an approved route or technical solution.
  7. Maintenance access Turn 14. Maintenance access is one relevant coordination question for any solution; no access or clearance arrangement is supplied here.
  8. coordination meeting Turn 16. Coordination meeting identifies the 13:00 review point and does not authorize installation or guarantee a completed solution.
  9. route approval Turn 18. Route approval is absent because neither trade has agreed a revised path; the query must preserve that status.
  10. unresolved interface Turn 20. Unresolved interface keeps the cross-trade conflict open until the actual project process establishes an agreed arrangement.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Elena: Dev, what is the ceiling issue for thirteen hundred? Give me the bay and references so I can put the right drawings on the agenda.
  2. Dev: There's a route conflict at C3. E6 shows a cable tray in the same space where the mechanical layout shows a duct.
  3. Elena: Is that what the drawings show, or are you reporting an installed clash? Those need different descriptions in the coordination record.
  4. Dev: I'm comparing the electrical layout against the mechanical layout. The case doesn't establish an installed clash or authorize any physical change.
  5. Elena: Has either team agreed another path in a separate discussion? I'd rather check that before reopening something already settled.

  8. Dev: A joint review is needed. Either drawing alone would omit the other service and conceal the interface we need to resolve.

  10. Dev: No trade priority is established. I won't assign the move to mechanical or promise an electrical change before the coordinated review.

  14. Dev: Correct. Maintenance access is one of those questions. This issue note doesn't provide a complete alternative arrangement for the team to install.

Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Two services in bay C3

An electrician asks the ceiling coordinator for a joint review.

  1. Electrician: Drawing E6 puts our cable tray, not conduit, in the duct space shown for C3.
  2. Ceiling coordinator: Are both layouts current, or might one be an older issue in the folder?
  3. Electrician: I've checked our current issue; the mechanical lead needs to confirm their layout status.
  4. Ceiling coordinator: Bring both references to the one o'clock meeting so we can establish that first.
  5. Electrician: Please include both trade leads. This needs a coordinated route decision, not a verbal guess.
  6. Ceiling coordinator: I agree. Can you mark the affected bay without proposing an installation change yourself?
  7. Electrician: Yes, I'll highlight the overlap and identify the drawing references for everyone attending.
  8. Ceiling coordinator: The client is asking whether the electrical team has already changed its route on site.
  9. Electrician: No change is agreed. Please describe this as a drawing conflict awaiting joint review.
  10. Ceiling coordinator: I will, and the meeting record will identify the decision needed and whoever accepts follow-up.
Access is shared, not assumed

An electrician and site manager discuss access above a shop counter.

  1. Electrician: We requested the counter area tomorrow morning. Has that access window been confirmed?
  2. Site manager: The shop manager offered nine until eleven, but another contractor has the same window.
  3. Electrician: Then both crews need a coordinated arrangement before either treats the area as available.
  4. Site manager: Could you accept eleven until one if the shop manager agrees to that change?
  5. Electrician: Possibly, but I'll check crew availability before committing to the revised period.
  6. Site manager: Please also say whether your visit needs the whole counter area or a defined section.
  7. Electrician: I'll identify the planned work area for review, without assuming another crew can work alongside.
  8. Site manager: That will help the shop manager understand the disruption instead of hearing electrical work all day.
  9. Electrician: Once the boundaries and timing are agreed, I'll repeat them back to our crew.
  10. Site manager: I'll coordinate the two requests and confirm one shared access plan with the shop manager.
A route on paper is not an opening approval

An electrician responds to a builder's interpretation of a containment drawing.

  1. Electrician: The proposed containment route passes near this wall, but no opening detail is agreed.
  2. Builder: I thought the route line meant the opening location and size were already settled.
  3. Electrician: It identifies a proposed path, not every structural or construction detail along that path.
  4. Builder: Who needs the question so we avoid passing an incomplete instruction between crews?
  5. Electrician: The designer and relevant building specialist need the location and current containment information.
  6. Builder: I'll assemble those references and ask the site coordinator to arrange the review.
  7. Electrician: Please keep the drawing label proposed visible until a coordinated response is received.
  8. Builder: Understood. I'll tell our supervisor that route coordination remains open at this wall.
  9. Electrician: Thank you. That preserves the distinction between identifying a path and approving associated building work.
  10. Builder: I'll use that wording in the coordination discussion instead of saying the electrician approved an opening.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. REFERENCE
Course: Electricians' Workplace English
Professional audience: Electricians, electrical apprentices in supervised communication roles, electrical crew leads, and service coordinators
Scope: The cases and products are fictional. This book teaches English, not electrical testing, switching, isolation, diagnosis, installation, circuit identification, or code compliance. Follow actual training, authorization, procedures, manufacturer information, and applicable requirements. Client descriptions, labels, outage plans, and handover records do not prove equipment is de-energized or safe to use or work on. These dialogues authorize no electrical operation, relabeling, routing change, substitution, or added load.
Lesson 04: Coordinating containment and shared work areas
Original fictional case: Electrical drawing E6 places a cable tray in ceiling bay C3. The mechanical layout shows a duct in the same space. Electrician Dev reports the conflict to ceiling coordinator Elena before the coordination meeting at 13:00. Neither trade has agreed a revised route. Dev needs both drawings reviewed together. He must not announce an invented offset, assume one trade has priority, or treat the meeting time as authorization to install a changed route.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

REFERENCE
Course: Electricians' Workplace English
Professional audience: Electricians, electrical apprentices in supervised communication roles, electrical crew leads, and service coordinators
Scope: The cases and products are fictional. This book teaches English, not electrical testing, switching, isolation, diagnosis, installation, circuit identification, or code compliance. Follow actual training, authorization, procedures, manufacturer information, and applicable requirements. Client descriptions, labels, outage plans, and handover records do not prove equipment is de-energized or safe to use or work on. These dialogues authorize no electrical operation, relabeling, routing change, substitution, or added load.
Lesson 04: Coordinating containment and shared work areas
Original fictional case: Electrical drawing E6 places a cable tray in ceiling bay C3. The mechanical layout shows a duct in the same space. Electrician Dev reports the conflict to ceiling coordinator Elena before the coordination meeting at 13:00. Neither trade has agreed a revised route. Dev needs both drawings reviewed together. He must not announce an invented offset, assume one trade has priority, or treat the meeting time as authorization to install a changed route.
Language workshop: A tray and a duct in bay C3
Communication goal: Describe a cross-trade drawing conflict, request joint review, and preserve the distinction between a meeting and an approved route.
Language guidance: E6 places a cable tray in ceiling bay C3 where the mechanical layout shows a duct. The conflict is between the drawn arrangements; the case does not establish completed installation. No revised route or priority rule is agreed.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The clash is in ceiling bay C3. / E6 shows the cable tray in that space. / The mechanical layout shows a duct there. / The two drawn routes occupy the same space. / We need both drawings reviewed together. / Neither trade has agreed a revised route. / I am not assuming that one trade automatically takes priority. / No offset has been proposed and approved here. / The ceiling coordination meeting is at 13:00. / The meeting time does not authorize a routing change. / Clearances and access would need review for any proposed solution. / I am describing the drawings, not a completed site installation. / Cable tray and duct conflict at C3, with route revision unresolved. / The review needs to identify the agreed arrangement through the project process. / Please keep E6 and the mechanical layout attached to the query. / The interface remains unresolved pending coordinated review.
Vocabulary: cable tray: Support system used to carry cables along a route. / containment: Systems supporting or enclosing cables along their route. / conduit: Tube or pipe used as an electrical raceway. / trunking: Enclosed channel used to contain cables in relevant terminology. / raceway: Channel designed to contain electrical conductors or cables. / duct: Passage or enclosure conveying air in a mechanical system. / ceiling bay: Defined section of ceiling space used as a location reference. / ceiling void: Space above a ceiling surface. / mechanical layout: Drawing showing the arrangement of mechanical services. / electrical layout: Drawing showing the arrangement of electrical work. / route conflict: Incompatibility between proposed paths or occupied spaces. / spatial clash: Overlap or incompatible use of physical space. / clearance requirement: Space needed around a component for the relevant purpose. / maintenance access: Space or route needed for inspection or servicing. / support arrangement: Specified way a component is held in place. / coordinated drawing: Drawing incorporating agreed interfaces among relevant disciplines. / revised route: Changed path proposed or agreed for a service. / proposed offset: Suggested displacement from a stated route or position. / trade priority: Claimed or agreed precedence between work disciplines. / joint review: Assessment involving the relevant parties together. / coordination meeting: Discussion intended to resolve interfaces and arrangements. / route approval: Acceptance of a specified routing arrangement. / drawing overlay: Combined view of related drawings for comparison. / unresolved interface: Cross-discipline connection or conflict not yet settled.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; name E6, C3, the tray, and the mechanical duct distinctly. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Make 13:00 a meeting, not an installation start. Check and read the transfer without assigning either trade an unapproved revised route.
Partner: Elena: Ceiling coordinator / Dev: Electrician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the coordination summary. Include the electrical source, shared bay, meeting time, and unresolved route status.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Elena: Read the short agenda entry back. I need both services, the bay, and the outstanding decision together.
Dev: E6 tray and mechanical-layout duct share C3; route approval remains outstanding; both drawings need review at thirteen hundred. Neither trade has accepted a revision.
Elena: That gives the meeting a precise issue without assigning blame. I'll retain both sources with it.
Dev: I'll keep it as an unresolved interface until the project process records an agreed arrangement. The meeting and query don't themselves authorize a routing change.
END REFERENCE
05Communicating outage plans and statusCorrecting the proposed outage notice+

Your goal

Preserve the proposed scope and time window, correct an overstated notice, and distinguish facilities approval from electrical status.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

Correcting the proposed outage notice

Electrician Imani reviews an outage request with facilities coordinator Joel. The request proposes 18:00-19:00 for reception lighting only. Facilities has not approved it. A draft tenant notice incorrectly says the whole floor will be unavailable. No isolation status is supplied. Imani and Joel need to correct the notice scope and its proposed status without presenting the window as confirmed or giving operational instructions.

Joel: Facilities coordinator / Imani: Electrician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the request propose?

Answer and explanation

C. Reception lighting only, 18:00-19:00

The proposed request covers only reception lighting within the stated one-hour window.

2. What is wrong with the tenant notice?

Answer and explanation

D. It says the whole floor will be unavailable

The notice expands the impact beyond the reception-lighting scope of the request.

3. What remains unapproved?

Answer and explanation

C. The outage request

Facilities has not approved the proposed outage arrangement.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The proposal is 18:00-19:00 for reception lighting only. Facilities approval is pending. The notice wrongly expands the scope to the whole floor. Correct both the affected service and the unapproved status; do not call the request a confirmed outage.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
outage request
Proposal for an interruption of a defined service.submit an outage request
proposed window
Suggested period not yet approved or confirmed.state the proposed window
reception lighting
Lighting serving the reception area.identify the reception-lighting scope
affected service
Particular function covered by the proposed interruption.name the affected service
impact scope
Extent of the service or area affected as described.correct the impact scope
tenant notice
Communication informing occupants of a relevant building arrangement.review the tenant notice
draft notice
Preliminary communication not yet treated as final.correct the draft notice
facilities approval
Acceptance through the responsible facilities process.await facilities approval
approval status
Whether a proposal is accepted, pending, or rejected.state the approval status
confirmed window
Time period explicitly agreed through the relevant process.distinguish a confirmed window
planned interruption
Intended break in service delivery.describe the planned interruption
isolation
Separation from relevant energy sources through the applicable authorized process.distinguish isolation from a planned outage
isolation status
Verified state under the applicable electrical procedure.avoid inferring isolation status
operational instruction
Direction to perform an equipment or system action.distinguish a notice from an operational instruction
notice correction
Amendment of inaccurate communication.make a notice correction
whole-floor impact
Claim that an entire floor is affected.avoid an unsupported whole-floor impact
service boundary
Limit of the functions included in the request.preserve the service boundary
kilowatt-hour
Unit of energy equal to one kilowatt sustained for one hour.estimate kilowatt-hours
pending approval
Awaiting a decision rather than accepted.mark the request pending approval
communication release
Authorization or controlled action to issue a notice.confirm the communication release
scope readback
Repetition of the exact extent of a proposal.give a scope readback
status qualifier
Wording showing the level of certainty or approval.preserve the status qualifier
authorization
Permission for a specified action through the relevant process.distinguish authorization from discussion
revision record
Note showing how a communication or document changed.maintain a revision record

No isolation status is given. An outage request, even if later approved, is not proof of safe electrical status. The exercise concerns accurate communication, not switching, testing, isolation, restoration, or instructions for staff to operate equipment.

C Notice the language

Correct scope and status together

A notice can mislead through both its area description and its level of certainty. Correcting whole floor to reception lighting is not enough if the notice still presents an unapproved request as confirmed. Keep proposal, approval, communication, and verified operational status separate.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State the proposal
The request proposes 18:00-19:00.
Name the exact service
The scope is reception lighting only.
State the approval limit
Facilities has not approved the request.
Flag the wording error
The draft notice incorrectly says the whole floor will be unavailable.
Correct the scope
Replace that impact claim with reception lighting only.
Correct the certainty
Keep the window labeled proposed, not confirmed.
Separate two checks
We need accurate scope and accurate approval status.
Avoid an all-services claim
The request does not include every service on the floor.
Preserve the time range
The proposed start is 18:00 and the proposed end is 19:00.
Avoid a safety inference
The request gives no verified isolation status.
Separate planning and operation
A notice is not an electrical operating instruction.
Avoid premature release
This discussion does not authorize issuing a final notice.
Use a status qualifier
Proposed window, pending facilities approval.
Read back the correction
Reception lighting only, 18:00-19:00, approval pending.
Preserve the review route
The corrected wording still needs the relevant review and release process.
Close with the unresolved status
No confirmed outage or operational clearance is established here.
6 language and precision notes
Proposes
Marks an arrangement as requested rather than accepted.
Only
Limits the service scope to reception lighting.
Will be unavailable
Can incorrectly present a proposal as a definite future event.
Pending
Indicates no approval decision has yet been supplied.
Notice versus instruction
Occupant information does not direct electrical operations.
No status supplied
Prevents assumptions about energization or isolation from planning paperwork.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which wording preserves scope and status?

Answer and explanation

C. Proposed reception-lighting interruption, 18:00-19:00, pending facilities approval.

The wording retains the limited service, proposed window, and pending approval without an operational claim.

2. Why is changing whole floor alone insufficient?

Answer and explanation

C. The notice must also stop presenting the unapproved window as confirmed.

Accurate area wording does not fix an inaccurate claim of approval or certainty.

3. What does the request establish about isolation?

Answer and explanation

D. No verified isolation status is supplied

Planning information supplies no verified electrical status or safety clearance.

4. What is the purpose of this review?

Answer and explanation

D. Correct the communication and preserve the unresolved approval

The task is a communication review, not authorization or instructions for electrical operation.

E Conversations

Correcting the proposed outage notice

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Joel: Facilities coordinator / Imani: Electrician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • affected service
  • status qualifier
  • notice correction
  • isolation status
  • impact scope
  • scope readback
  • authorization
  • communication release
  • facilities approval
  • proposed window
  1. Joel
    The draft tenant notice says the whole floor will be unavailable from eighteen hundred to nineteen hundred. Is that actually your request?
  2. Imani
    No. The 1 is reception lighting only. Whole floor expands the impact beyond the request and needs correcting.
  3. Joel
    I'll change that. Can I then say reception lighting will be unavailable during the hour, or is will too definite?
  4. Imani
    It's too definite. 2 hasn't been given. The requested window remains proposed, not confirmed.
  5. Joel
    Then there are two problems: the notice includes too much of the building and presents an unaccepted request as a settled arrangement.
  6. Imani
    Exactly. Correcting the 3 alone won't make it accurate. Pending approval needs to remain visible beside the service description.
  7. Joel
    Read the requested times back as well. I don't want to change the window while fixing the wording.
  8. Imani
    The 4 is eighteen hundred to nineteen hundred. Those are requested times, not directions to begin or end an electrical operation.
  9. Joel
    Would proposed reception-lighting interruption, pending facilities approval work? I'd include the requested hour and keep only with the lighting scope.
  10. Imani
    Yes. That 5 makes the uncertainty explicit. It avoids presenting all services as affected or the window as accepted.
  11. Joel
    Should we mention that the equipment is isolated? People sometimes treat an outage announcement as proof the work area is already safe.
  12. Imani
    No 6 is supplied. Planning paperwork doesn't verify de-energization or safety, and the notice mustn't imply either.
  13. Joel
    Understood. We're correcting a proposal description, not giving tenants or the site team switching, testing, or restoration instructions.
  14. Imani
    Correct. This is a 7, not an operating procedure. Neither our discussion nor the revised sentence authorizes electrical action.
  15. Joel
    Does agreeing the sentence mean I can release a final notice now, or must it stay in the draft-review process?
  16. Imani
    8 remains separate. The wording needs the actual review and release process, and outage approval is still pending.
  17. Joel
    Give me the final short read-back so I can compare it with the request before sending it to the next reviewer.
  18. Imani
    The 9 is reception lighting only; proposed eighteen hundred to nineteen hundred; facilities approval pending. No whole-floor impact or verified isolation is established.
  19. Joel
    I'll retain those qualifiers. A corrected sentence mustn't make it look as though facilities has now approved the request.
  20. Imani
    Right. The 10 status is unchanged. We've identified communication corrections, not approved the outage or established an operational clearance.

Answers and explanations
  1. affected service Turn 2. Affected service is reception lighting only; the request does not cover every service or the whole floor.
  2. facilities approval Turn 4. Facilities approval has not been given, so the proposed window cannot be presented as a confirmed outage.
  3. impact scope Turn 6. Impact scope limits the proposal to reception lighting, but correcting it does not resolve the separate pending-approval issue.
  4. proposed window Turn 8. Proposed window is 18:00-19:00, with neither endpoint constituting a confirmed operational arrangement.
  5. status qualifier Turn 10. Status qualifier makes the proposal and pending approval explicit, preventing the wording from implying a settled arrangement.
  6. isolation status Turn 12. Isolation status is not supplied by the request or this conversation, so no de-energization or safety conclusion can be inferred.
  7. notice correction Turn 14. Notice correction amends scope and certainty in the communication; it does not direct an electrical operation.
  8. communication release Turn 16. Communication release remains subject to the actual review process; correcting a draft does not authorize issuing a final notice.
  9. scope readback Turn 18. Scope readback preserves reception lighting only, the proposed 18:00-19:00 window, and pending facilities approval together.
  10. authorization Turn 20. Authorization for the outage or electrical operation is not created by this notice review; the actual approval processes still apply.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Joel: The draft tenant notice says the whole floor will be unavailable from eighteen hundred to nineteen hundred. Is that actually your request?
  2. Imani: No. The affected service is reception lighting only. Whole floor expands the impact beyond the request and needs correcting.
  3. Joel: I'll change that. Can I then say reception lighting will be unavailable during the hour, or is will too definite?
  4. Imani: It's too definite. facilities approval hasn't been given. The requested window remains proposed, not confirmed.
  5. Joel: Then there are two problems: the notice includes too much of the building and presents an unaccepted request as a settled arrangement.
  6. Imani: Exactly. Correcting the impact scope alone won't make it accurate. Pending approval needs to remain visible beside the service description.
  7. Joel: Read the requested times back as well. I don't want to change the window while fixing the wording.
  8. Imani: The proposed window is eighteen hundred to nineteen hundred. Those are requested times, not directions to begin or end an electrical operation.
  9. Joel: Would proposed reception-lighting interruption, pending facilities approval work? I'd include the requested hour and keep only with the lighting scope.
  10. Imani: Yes. That status qualifier makes the uncertainty explicit. It avoids presenting all services as affected or the window as accepted.
  11. Joel: Should we mention that the equipment is isolated? People sometimes treat an outage announcement as proof the work area is already safe.
  12. Imani: No isolation status is supplied. Planning paperwork doesn't verify de-energization or safety, and the notice mustn't imply either.
  13. Joel: Understood. We're correcting a proposal description, not giving tenants or the site team switching, testing, or restoration instructions.
  14. Imani: Correct. This is a notice correction, not an operating procedure. Neither our discussion nor the revised sentence authorizes electrical action.
  15. Joel: Does agreeing the sentence mean I can release a final notice now, or must it stay in the draft-review process?
  16. Imani: communication release remains separate. The wording needs the actual review and release process, and outage approval is still pending.
  17. Joel: Give me the final short read-back so I can compare it with the request before sending it to the next reviewer.
  18. Imani: The scope readback is reception lighting only; proposed eighteen hundred to nineteen hundred; facilities approval pending. No whole-floor impact or verified isolation is established.
  19. Joel: I'll retain those qualifiers. A corrected sentence mustn't make it look as though facilities has now approved the request.
  20. Imani: Right. The authorization status is unchanged. We've identified communication corrections, not approved the outage or established an operational clearance.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Correcting the tenant notice

An electrician and facilities representative review the draft outage notice.

  1. Electrician: The draft says whole floor, but our request covers reception lighting only.
  2. Facilities representative: Thank you. Is the six-to-seven period confirmed, or still awaiting our approval?
  3. Electrician: The outage window remains proposed. Please preserve that status in the revised shutdown notice.
  4. Facilities representative: We can approve that period now, provided the described affected services remain unchanged.
  5. Electrician: I'll record that planning approval and correct the area before the notice is issued.
  6. Facilities representative: Does this mean the equipment is isolated and the crew can begin work?
  7. Electrician: No. This conversation confirms a booking, not technical status or permission to proceed.
  8. Facilities representative: Understood. The responsible electrical lead will communicate those matters through the site arrangements.
  9. Electrician: Please have the tenant contact repeat back the affected area when they receive the notice.
  10. Facilities representative: I'll confirm reception lighting and the agreed hour, without making any broader electrical-status claim.
The tenant names an overlooked dependency

An electrician discusses a proposed interruption with an office contact.

  1. Electrician: The proposed notice names the lobby lighting. Do you have any operational concerns to raise?
  2. Office contact: Our evening training group uses the lobby for registration at the proposed time.
  3. Electrician: That's useful scheduling information. Has the facilities coordinator received that conflict from you?
  4. Office contact: Not yet. I thought an electrical notice meant the date couldn't be discussed.
  5. Electrician: This one is proposed. Let's take the registration conflict to the coordinator before confirming.
  6. Office contact: Could we request a later window, after the group moves into its room?
  7. Electrician: We can request it, but crew availability and the revised site plan still need checking.
  8. Office contact: I'll provide the registration time rather than simply saying the whole evening is impossible.
  9. Electrician: I'll include that specific constraint when asking the coordinator to review the proposed period.
  10. Office contact: Thank you. Please send the confirmed arrangement before we change the training invitation.
No restoration time has been confirmed

An electrician relays a status update to a building manager.

  1. Electrician: The electrical lead's update says the review is continuing; no restoration time is confirmed.
  2. Building manager: The tenant remembers seven o'clock from the earlier planned window and expects that deadline.
  3. Electrician: We need to distinguish the earlier plan from the current status they have reported.
  4. Building manager: Can you promise another half hour so I have something definite to tell them?
  5. Electrician: I can't invent that estimate. I can ask when the next status update is due.
  6. Building manager: Please do. The tenants need a contact and a clear account of what remains unknown.
  7. Electrician: The lead has agreed to another update at seven fifteen, without promising restoration then.
  8. Building manager: I'll tell them seven fifteen is the update time, not the service-return time.
  9. Electrician: Exactly. That preserves the distinction and avoids replacing one expectation with another unsupported promise.
  10. Building manager: I'll relay the current status now and keep the tenant contact available for that update.

  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20; separate facilities approval, notice release, and electrical status.

Correct scope and certainty

Answer and explanation

Answer and explanation

A. 19:00

Nineteen hundred is the proposed endpoint in the request.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. REFERENCE
Course: Electricians' Workplace English
Professional audience: Electricians, electrical apprentices in supervised communication roles, electrical crew leads, and service coordinators
Scope: The cases and products are fictional. This book teaches English, not electrical testing, switching, isolation, diagnosis, installation, circuit identification, or code compliance. Follow actual training, authorization, procedures, manufacturer information, and applicable requirements. Client descriptions, labels, outage plans, and handover records do not prove equipment is de-energized or safe to use or work on. These dialogues authorize no electrical operation, relabeling, routing change, substitution, or added load.
Lesson 05: Communicating outage plans and status
Original fictional case: Electrician Imani reviews an outage request with facilities coordinator Joel. The request proposes 18:00-19:00 for reception lighting only. Facilities has not approved it. A draft tenant notice incorrectly says the whole floor will be unavailable. No isolation status is supplied. Imani and Joel need to correct the notice scope and its proposed status without presenting the window as confirmed or giving operational instructions.
Language workshop: Correcting the proposed outage notice
Communication goal: Preserve the proposed scope and time window, correct an overstated notice, and distinguish facilities approval from electrical status.
Language guidance: The proposal is 18:00-19:00 for reception lighting only. Facilities approval is pending. The notice wrongly expands the scope to the whole floor. Correct both the affected service and the unapproved status; do not call the request a confirmed outage.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The request proposes 18:00-19:00. / The scope is reception lighting only. / Facilities has not approved the request. / The draft notice incorrectly says the whole floor will be unavailable. / Replace that impact claim with reception lighting only. / Keep the window labeled proposed, not confirmed. / We need accurate scope and accurate approval status. / The request does not include every service on the floor. / The proposed start is 18:00 and the proposed end is 19:00. / The request gives no verified isolation status. / A notice is not an electrical operating instruction. / This discussion does not authorize issuing a final notice. / Proposed window, pending facilities approval. / Reception lighting only, 18:00-19:00, approval pending. / The corrected wording still needs the relevant review and release process. / No confirmed outage or operational clearance is established here.
Vocabulary: outage request: Proposal for an interruption of a defined service. / proposed window: Suggested period not yet approved or confirmed. / reception lighting: Lighting serving the reception area. / affected service: Particular function covered by the proposed interruption. / impact scope: Extent of the service or area affected as described. / tenant notice: Communication informing occupants of a relevant building arrangement. / draft notice: Preliminary communication not yet treated as final. / facilities approval: Acceptance through the responsible facilities process. / approval status: Whether a proposal is accepted, pending, or rejected. / confirmed window: Time period explicitly agreed through the relevant process. / planned interruption: Intended break in service delivery. / isolation: Separation from relevant energy sources through the applicable authorized process. / isolation status: Verified state under the applicable electrical procedure. / operational instruction: Direction to perform an equipment or system action. / notice correction: Amendment of inaccurate communication. / whole-floor impact: Claim that an entire floor is affected. / service boundary: Limit of the functions included in the request. / kilowatt-hour: Unit of energy equal to one kilowatt sustained for one hour. / pending approval: Awaiting a decision rather than accepted. / communication release: Authorization or controlled action to issue a notice. / scope readback: Repetition of the exact extent of a proposal. / status qualifier: Wording showing the level of certainty or approval. / authorization: Permission for a specified action through the relevant process. / revision record: Note showing how a communication or document changed.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 with reception lighting only and proposed clearly stressed. Switch roles for turns 11-20; separate facilities approval, notice release, and electrical status. Check and read the corrected notice without turning its window into an operating instruction.
Partner: Joel: Facilities coordinator / Imani: Electrician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the notice-review exchange. Retain the exact service and proposed time range, while making the approval limit explicit.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Joel: Give me the final short read-back so I can compare it with the request before sending it to the next reviewer.
Imani: The scope readback is reception lighting only; proposed eighteen hundred to nineteen hundred; facilities approval pending. No whole-floor impact or verified isolation is established.
Joel: I'll retain those qualifiers. A corrected sentence mustn't make it look as though facilities has now approved the request.
Imani: Right. The authorization status is unchanged. We've identified communication corrections, not approved the outage or established an operational clearance.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
