06Reporting findings and uncertaintyNot seen today is not fault-free+

Your goal

Distinguish a reported intermittent symptom from a limited visit observation and avoid turning a pending report into an unsupported assurance.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Not seen today is not fault-free

Client Mira reported occasional flickering in display bay 2. Electrician Ellis has a visit note stating that flickering was not observed during the visit. No cause is established, and the technical report is pending review. Mira asks whether the installation is now fault-free. Ellis must explain the limited finding without dismissing the earlier report, inventing a repair, or asserting that the installation is safe or free of defects.

Mira: Client / Ellis: Electrician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What did Mira report?

Answer and explanation

C. Occasional flickering in display bay 2

Mira reports an occasional symptom in a specific display bay.

2. What does the visit note state?

Answer and explanation

D. Flickering was not observed during the visit

The note describes what was not observed during that visit, not a diagnosis or repair.

3. What is the report status?

Answer and explanation

A. Pending review, with no cause established

The technical report is still awaiting review and no cause has been established.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Mira reported occasional flickering, while the visit note says none was observed during that attendance. Those statements do not contradict each other. The note does not prove that the symptom never occurs, that a cause has been identified, or that a repair has been completed.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
flickering
Visible variation in light output perceived as fluctuating light.report occasional flickering
intermittent symptom
Problem that appears at intervals rather than continuously.describe an intermittent symptom
display bay
Defined display area within a premises.identify display bay 2
visit note
Record of information from a particular attendance.quote the visit note
observation period
Time during which a condition was observed or monitored.define the observation period
not observed
Wording stating that a condition was not seen during the specified period.preserve not-observed wording
reported history
Earlier account of events or symptoms.retain the reported history
technical report
Document recording the relevant professional information and findings.submit the technical report
pending review
Awaiting examination or approval through the relevant process.state pending-review status
established cause
Explanation supported by the relevant assessment.distinguish an established cause
fault-free claim
Assertion that no faults are present.avoid an unsupported fault-free claim
evidence limit
Boundary of what the available information supports.explain the evidence limit
repeat occurrence
Another instance of a previously reported symptom.record a repeat occurrence
observation finding
Statement about what was seen during an examination or visit.report the observation finding
diagnostic conclusion
Conclusion identifying a technical problem or its cause.distinguish a diagnostic conclusion
repair record
Documentation of work actually carried out to correct an issue.verify the repair record
defect
Condition that fails an applicable requirement or expected function.assess a reported defect
operational clearance
Authorization or verified status for use under the actual process.avoid inferring operational clearance
qualified statement
Claim limited to the scope supported by the evidence.give a qualified statement
absence of evidence
Lack of a finding, not automatically proof that a condition is absent.explain absence of evidence
symptom frequency
How often the reported condition occurs.clarify symptom frequency
review outcome
Result of the relevant report assessment.await the review outcome
case status
Current stage of the reported issue and its follow-up.report the case status
unsupported reassurance
Comforting claim not established by the available evidence.avoid unsupported reassurance

No cause is established, and technical-report review is pending. Keep fault-free and safe-to-use claims out of the summary unless the actual authorized process establishes them. This language exercise gives no diagnostic procedure, testing instruction, or operational clearance.

C Notice the language

Acknowledge both accounts without forcing them to agree

An intermittent symptom may be absent during a visit. The client report and the visit observation can therefore coexist. Explain the observation period and the unresolved cause, rather than treating the client as mistaken or upgrading limited evidence into complete reassurance.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the history
You reported occasional flickering in display bay 2.
Quote the limited finding
Flickering was not observed during the visit.
Keep the time boundary
That statement applies to the visit observation period.
Explain coexistence
An occasional symptom may not appear during every visit.
Avoid dismissing the client
The note does not show that your earlier report was wrong.
Keep cause open
No cause has been established.
Keep report status open
The technical report is pending review.
Reject an overbroad conclusion
I cannot turn that note into a fault-free claim.
Avoid inventing work
No completed repair is established by this information.
Separate finding and diagnosis
Not observed is an observation statement, not a diagnosis.
Avoid a safety inference
The note does not establish operational clearance.
Preserve the location
The reported symptom concerns display bay 2.
Use qualified wording
No flickering observed during this visit; cause unresolved.
Avoid an exact frequency claim
Occasional does not provide a precise interval.
Read back the status
Your report remains recorded, the visit observation is limited, and review is pending.
Close without false certainty
I can explain the record, but I cannot promise the issue is absent.
6 language and precision notes
Occasional
Indicates intermittent occurrence without specifying a precise frequency.
During the visit
Limits the observation to a particular attendance period.
Not observed versus absent
A missing observation does not prove the condition never occurs.
Pending review
A report stage, not a completed technical conclusion.
Now fault-free
A stronger claim than the supplied visit note supports.
Established
Requires supporting assessment, not a plausible guess.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which explanation reconciles the two accounts?

Answer and explanation

A. Occasional flickering may not appear during a particular visit.

The reported intermittent history can coexist with no flickering observed during one attendance.

2. Which summary is supported?

Answer and explanation

C. No flickering observed during the visit; cause unestablished; report review pending.

The summary preserves the actual observation, unresolved cause, and report stage.

3. Which statement invents a repair?

Answer and explanation

B. The issue is fixed because it did not flicker while we were there.

No repair or verified correction is supplied, so symptom absence during the visit cannot prove a fix.

4. What should remain in the record?

Answer and explanation

C. Both Mira's reported history and the limited visit observation

Keeping both accounts preserves the history and the actual limits of the visit evidence.

Not seen today is not fault-free

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mira: Client / Ellis: Electrician

  2. Ellis
    It would. The 1 is limited to that attendance. The note doesn't establish a fault-free installation or identify a cause.
  3. Mira
    It happens occasionally in display bay two, not continuously. That's why I was concerned a visit might not catch it.
  4. Ellis
    An 2 may not appear during a particular attendance. Your report and the visit note can both be accurate.
  5. Mira
    I'm glad that doesn't mean my report is being dismissed. I don't want anyone to read it as saying I imagined the problem.
  6. Ellis
    We'll retain the 3 of occasional flickering in display bay two. The note doesn't disprove it or establish conditions in every other area.
  7. Mira
    Has anyone identified a component or explanation? I'd like to distinguish a possibility from a conclusion the technical team has actually reached.
  8. Ellis
    There's no 4 in this information. I can't name a failed component or offer a diagnosis that the report hasn't established.
  9. Mira
    Is the technical report final? Our manager will probably ask whether this is the accepted conclusion or just the current visit record.
  10. Ellis
    It's 5. I can explain the visit note, but I shouldn't describe the report as finalized or approved.
  11. Mira
    And we can't say a repair fixed it merely because it didn't flicker during the visit? I haven't seen anything recording corrective work.
  12. Ellis
    No 6 confirming completed work is supplied. Absence of the symptom during attendance doesn't establish that a repair happened or succeeded.
  13. Mira
    Could you give me a short accurate summary? I need to avoid both closing the issue prematurely and exaggerating the findings.
  14. Ellis
    Use a 7: occasional flickering reported in display bay two; none observed during the visit; cause not established; report awaiting review.
  15. Mira
    That preserves my account without claiming the electrician saw the flickering. It also explains why the two records aren't contradictory.
  16. Ellis
    Yes. The 8 matters. A visit-specific observation mustn't become a universal assurance about the whole installation.
  17. Mira
    Does that summary give anyone permission to use or work on the equipment? I want to be careful with words like cleared.
  18. Ellis
    It gives no 9. The actual authorized process governs those questions; this conversation isn't permission for an electrical action.
  19. Mira
    I'll tell the manager the cause remains unresolved and review is pending. I won't call it fixed or fault-free from this note.
  20. Ellis
    That preserves the 10. Your report stays alongside the limited observation, and no unsupported diagnosis or repair claim closes the issue.

Answers and explanations
  1. observation period Turn 2. Observation period limits the finding to the visit, not every time the installation has operated.
  2. intermittent symptom Turn 4. Intermittent symptom describes occasional flickering, which may not appear during a particular attendance.
  3. reported history Turn 6. Reported history preserves Mira's earlier account instead of replacing it with a claim that she was mistaken.
  4. established cause Turn 8. Established cause is absent; the supplied information does not support naming a failed component or a diagnosis.
  5. pending review Turn 10. Pending review describes the current report stage and does not imply an approved technical conclusion.
  6. repair record Turn 12. Repair record confirming completed corrective work is not supplied; absence of observed flickering is not proof of repair.
  7. qualified statement Turn 14. Qualified statement limits the summary to the actual visit observation, unresolved cause, and pending report review.
  8. evidence limit Turn 16. Evidence limit prevents a visit-specific observation from becoming a universal fault-free assurance or a technical diagnosis.
  9. operational clearance Turn 18. Operational clearance is not established by the visit note or language summary; the actual authorized process still governs use and work.
  10. case status Turn 20. Case status remains unresolved with review pending; the client report and limited observation both stay in the record.

Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Not observed is not ruled out

An electrician explains the display-bay visit note to the client.

  1. Electrician: We didn't observe flickering during the visit to display bay two today.
  2. Client: Does that mean the problem has gone away and we can close the request?
  3. Electrician: Not necessarily. An intermittent fault may not appear during a particular observation period.
  4. Client: I was worried you thought I imagined it because it wasn't happening then.
  5. Electrician: Your report remains in the service history, with its location and timing clearly attributed.
  6. Client: Have you decided whether the driver is causing it and needs replacing?
  7. Electrician: No cause is confirmed, and the technical report is still awaiting review.
  8. Client: Please keep the request open until the reviewer explains what the findings actually establish.
  9. Electrician: I'll ask for that interpretation and relay the outcome without promising a particular repair.
  10. Client: Thank you. That addresses my concern without turning today's quiet visit into proof of no fault.
A result without a matching reference

An electrician queries a report identifier with a test-report coordinator.

  1. Electrician: This test report lists C12, but our enquiry concerns the display-bay circuit C21.
  2. Report coordinator: The digits may have been transposed. Can you simply correct the report label?
  3. Electrician: We need the report author to verify the tested reference before changing the record.
  4. Report coordinator: You're right. A plausible typing error doesn't establish which circuit the results belong to.
  5. Electrician: Please ask the author to reconcile the source record and issue any correction themselves.
  6. Report coordinator: I'll preserve the received report and identify the reference mismatch in the query.
  7. Electrician: The client is asking whether the listed insulation resistance result proves their concern is resolved.
  8. Report coordinator: That result can't be attributed to their circuit until the reference and interpretation are verified.
  9. Electrician: I'll explain that the report linkage is pending, without interpreting the measurement myself.
  10. Report coordinator: I'll contact the author and return the corrected or confirmed record with its review status.
The symptom returned after the visit

An electrician takes a follow-up account from a shop supervisor.

  1. Electrician: You reported the display flickered again after our visit. When did you notice it?
  2. Shop supervisor: At about five thirty, near the same display bay as before yesterday's appointment.
  3. Electrician: I'll add that account to the existing service history as a later report.
  4. Shop supervisor: Does the recurrence mean the earlier electrician's report was necessarily wrong about the visit?
  5. Electrician: No. A later event doesn't change what was observed during that earlier period.
  6. Shop supervisor: That makes sense. I want the new information considered, not the old record erased.
  7. Electrician: I'll link both accounts and ask the technical reviewer to assess the updated history.
  8. Shop supervisor: Should we describe the cause as confirmed now that the symptom happened twice?
  9. Electrician: Repeated reports establish recurrence, not a particular cause. That conclusion still needs technical assessment.
  10. Shop supervisor: I'll use recurrence in my update and leave the diagnosis to the reviewer.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Not seen today is not fault-free
  1. Read turns 1-10, retaining Mira's report alongside the limited visit observation.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20; stress not observed rather than fixed or fault-free.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Keep the observation within its limits

Complete the client summary. Retain the intermittent history, the visit-specific observation, the unknown cause, and pending review.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Client: "I reported occasional ___ in display bay 2."

Answer and explanation

A. flickering

Flickering is the reported intermittent symptom in the specified display area.

2. Electrician: "It was not ___ during the visit."

Answer and explanation

A. observed

Not observed limits the finding to that visit rather than proving permanent absence.

3. Client: "No cause is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. established

No assessment result in the case identifies the cause.

4. Electrician: "The technical report is pending ___."

Answer and explanation

D. review

Review remains pending, so the report is not presented as finalized.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Course: Electricians' Workplace English
Professional audience: Electricians, electrical apprentices in supervised communication roles, electrical crew leads, and service coordinators
Scope: The cases and products are fictional. This book teaches English, not electrical testing, switching, isolation, diagnosis, installation, circuit identification, or code compliance. Follow actual training, authorization, procedures, manufacturer information, and applicable requirements. Client descriptions, labels, outage plans, and handover records do not prove equipment is de-energized or safe to use or work on. These dialogues authorize no electrical operation, relabeling, routing change, substitution, or added load.
Lesson 06: Reporting findings and uncertainty
Original fictional case: Client Mira reported occasional flickering in display bay 2. Electrician Ellis has a visit note stating that flickering was not observed during the visit. No cause is established, and the technical report is pending review. Mira asks whether the installation is now fault-free. Ellis must explain the limited finding without dismissing the earlier report, inventing a repair, or asserting that the installation is safe or free of defects.
Language workshop: Not seen today is not fault-free
Communication goal: Distinguish a reported intermittent symptom from a limited visit observation and avoid turning a pending report into an unsupported assurance.
Language guidance: Mira reported occasional flickering, while the visit note says none was observed during that attendance. Those statements do not contradict each other. The note does not prove that the symptom never occurs, that a cause has been identified, or that a repair has been completed.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): You reported occasional flickering in display bay 2. / Flickering was not observed during the visit. / That statement applies to the visit observation period. / An occasional symptom may not appear during every visit. / The note does not show that your earlier report was wrong. / No cause has been established. / The technical report is pending review. / I cannot turn that note into a fault-free claim. / No completed repair is established by this information. / Not observed is an observation statement, not a diagnosis. / The note does not establish operational clearance. / The reported symptom concerns display bay 2. / No flickering observed during this visit; cause unresolved. / Occasional does not provide a precise interval. / Your report remains recorded, the visit observation is limited, and review is pending. / I can explain the record, but I cannot promise the issue is absent.
Vocabulary: flickering: Visible variation in light output perceived as fluctuating light. / intermittent symptom: Problem that appears at intervals rather than continuously. / display bay: Defined display area within a premises. / visit note: Record of information from a particular attendance. / observation period: Time during which a condition was observed or monitored. / not observed: Wording stating that a condition was not seen during the specified period. / reported history: Earlier account of events or symptoms. / technical report: Document recording the relevant professional information and findings. / pending review: Awaiting examination or approval through the relevant process. / established cause: Explanation supported by the relevant assessment. / fault-free claim: Assertion that no faults are present. / evidence limit: Boundary of what the available information supports. / repeat occurrence: Another instance of a previously reported symptom. / observation finding: Statement about what was seen during an examination or visit. / diagnostic conclusion: Conclusion identifying a technical problem or its cause. / repair record: Documentation of work actually carried out to correct an issue. / defect: Condition that fails an applicable requirement or expected function. / operational clearance: Authorization or verified status for use under the actual process. / qualified statement: Claim limited to the scope supported by the evidence. / absence of evidence: Lack of a finding, not automatically proof that a condition is absent. / symptom frequency: How often the reported condition occurs. / review outcome: Result of the relevant report assessment. / case status: Current stage of the reported issue and its follow-up. / unsupported reassurance: Comforting claim not established by the available evidence.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10, retaining Mira's report alongside the limited visit observation. Switch roles for turns 11-20; stress not observed rather than fixed or fault-free. Check and read the transfer. Keep review pending and the cause unestablished.
Partner: Mira: Client / Ellis: Electrician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the client summary. Retain the intermittent history, the visit-specific observation, the unknown cause, and pending review.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mira: Does that summary give anyone permission to use or work on the equipment? I want to be careful with words like cleared.
Ellis: It gives no operational clearance. The actual authorized process governs those questions; this conversation isn't permission for an electrical action.
Mira: I'll tell the manager the cause remains unresolved and review is pending. I won't call it fixed or fault-free from this note.
Ellis: That preserves the case status. Your report stays alongside the limited observation, and no unsupported diagnosis or repair claim closes the issue.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Assessing added equipment and substitutionsA second machine changes the question+

Your goal

Request missing equipment information and explain the need for revised assessment and quotation without confirming capacity, price, or timing.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

A second machine changes the question

After electrical scope is agreed for a café, owner Hana adds a second espresso machine and asks electrician Luis to include it at the original price and completion date. Equipment data for the added machine are unavailable. Luis can request the data and arrange a revised assessment and quotation. Electrical capacity and installation requirements are not established. The discussion must not assume the new machine matches the first, fits the existing provision, or can be added without cost or schedule effects.

Hana: Café owner / Luis: Electrician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What changed after scope agreement?

Answer and explanation

B. A second espresso machine was added

Hana adds the second machine after the electrical scope has been agreed.

2. What information is missing?

Answer and explanation

D. Equipment data for the added machine

The case states that the additional machine's equipment data are unavailable.

3. What can Luis offer?

Answer and explanation

A. Request data and arrange revised assessment and quotation

The supported route is data collection and revised review, not technical or commercial approval.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The second machine is a scope addition, not an already assessed part of the original work. Equipment data are missing, so capacity and installation requirements remain unknown. The machine category alone does not establish that it matches the first unit.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
equipment data
Product-specific technical information needed for assessment.request the equipment data
espresso machine
Coffee-making equipment forming the added request in this case.identify the second espresso machine
additional load
Extra electrical demand associated with added equipment.assess the additional load
rated input
Manufacturer-stated input requirement under specified conditions.confirm the rated input
nameplate
Product marking showing identification and relevant ratings.obtain recorded nameplate information
manufacturer specification
Technical requirements or attributes supplied by the manufacturer.review the manufacturer specification
supply requirement
Electrical supply characteristics required by the equipment.establish the supply requirement
capacity assessment
Evaluation of whether the relevant provision can meet the proposed demand.arrange a capacity assessment
existing provision
Electrical arrangement already included or available for the project.review the existing provision
installation requirement
Condition or detail needed for the specified installation.establish installation requirements
revised assessment
Updated review accounting for changed information or scope.request a revised assessment
revised quotation
Updated price proposal for the changed defined work.prepare a revised quotation
original scope
Work included before the new request.preserve the original scope
scope addition
Extra item proposed after the agreed scope.record the scope addition
active power
Electrical power associated with net energy transfer, expressed in watts or kilowatts.report active power
price commitment
Agreed promise about cost.avoid an unsupported price commitment
schedule impact
Effect of a change on timing.assess the schedule impact
technical suitability
Whether the proposed arrangement meets relevant technical needs.verify technical suitability
product substitution
Replacement of a specified item with a different product.review a product substitution
like-for-like claim
Assertion that a replacement or addition has equivalent relevant characteristics.verify a like-for-like claim
apparent power
For a single-phase load, RMS voltage times RMS current, expressed in volt-amperes.distinguish apparent power
approval boundary
Limit of what has actually been authorized.preserve the approval boundary
change request
Proposal to alter the agreed work or requirements.submit a change request
assessment outcome
Result reached after the required review.await the assessment outcome

Hana requests the original price and completion date, but Luis has not accepted those terms for the addition. Request the relevant data through the actual process and arrange revised assessment and quotation. Do not ask the client to test, connect, or operate equipment.

C Notice the language

Treat an added appliance as a defined change request

A client may see an extra machine as a simple addition to a familiar plan. Explain which information is missing and how it affects the assessment route. Preserve the commercial request without accepting it or inventing the technical answer.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the addition
You want to add a second espresso machine.
Name the scope change
That machine was added after the electrical scope was agreed.
Identify the missing information
We do not have the equipment data for the added unit.
Request the data
Please provide the relevant product information through the usual project process.
Avoid assuming equivalence
I cannot assume it has the same requirements as the first machine.
Keep capacity open
Available capacity has not been established for the addition.
Keep installation open
The installation requirements still need assessment.
Offer the review route
I can arrange a revised assessment and quotation.
Preserve the price request
I understand you want to keep the original price.
Avoid accepting the price
I cannot confirm unchanged cost for the added machine.
Preserve the timing request
I understand the original completion date matters to you.
Avoid accepting the date
I cannot promise the same date before the change is assessed.
Separate two decisions
Technical suitability and commercial terms both remain open.
Avoid operational instructions
This information request does not authorize connection or testing.
Read back the change
Second machine requested; data missing; capacity, requirements, price, and timing unresolved.
Close with the next step
We need the data for the revised assessment and quotation.
6 language and precision notes
Added after
Establishes why the extra machine is outside the already agreed scope.
Same kind of machine
Does not establish identical ratings or requirements.
Request versus commitment
Asking for the original price or date does not mean those terms are accepted.
Revised
Indicates that the review must account for changed scope.
Not established
Leaves the answer open rather than predicting failure or approval.
Both remain open
Keeps technical assessment and commercial agreement distinct.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response preserves the facts?

Answer and explanation

A. We need the added machine's data for revised assessment and quotation before confirming terms.

Missing data and changed scope require review before technical or commercial commitments can be established.

2. Which assumption is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

C. The second machine has the same requirements as the first.

No product-specific information establishes equivalence between the two machines.

3. What is the status of unchanged price and date?

Answer and explanation

A. Requested by Hana but not committed by Luis

Hana's commercial preference remains a request while the additional work is unassessed.

4. What should the next handoff contain?

Answer and explanation

C. Added machine, missing data, requested terms, and need for revised assessment and quotation

The handoff preserves the actual change and unresolved questions without technical or commercial invention.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

A second machine changes the question

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Hana: Café owner / Luis: Electrician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • supply requirement
  • approval boundary
  • capacity assessment
  • schedule impact
  • revised quotation
  • price commitment
  • equipment data
  • scope addition
  • like-for-like claim
  • change request
  1. Hana
    We're adding a second espresso machine. Can the work still fit the original price and completion date, or does this need separate review?
  2. Luis
    I understand the request, but it is a 1. The second machine was added after we agreed the electrical scope, and its equipment data are not available yet.
  3. Hana
    I assumed another espresso machine would need similar provision. Is the category enough information, or do you need its specific details?
  4. Luis
    The category does not establish the 2. I cannot assume the added unit has the same relevant characteristics or that the existing provision is suitable.
  5. Hana
    Which documents should the project team send? I don't want ratings guessed from a photograph or a general product name.
  6. Luis
    We need the relevant 3 for the added unit through the usual project process. This is an information request, not a request for you to test or connect anything.
  7. Hana
    Once the data arrive, you'll review capacity and installation requirements? Neither answer is established for the addition yet?
  8. Luis
    A 4 forms part of the revised review. At present, capacity and installation requirements are not established, so I cannot give a yes-or-no technical approval.
  9. Hana
    I'd like the original price retained, but record that as my request. You haven't quoted or accepted the extra work.
  10. Luis
    Correct. There is no unchanged 5 for the addition. We can preserve your preference without recording it as something we have already accepted.
  11. Hana
    Please keep the original completion date in the review brief. Other work depends on it, although this change's effect is still unknown.
  12. Luis
    I will include it, but the 6 remains open. I cannot promise the same completion date for changed work before the requirements and arrangements have been assessed.
  13. Hana
    Then a technical answer won't settle everything. I need defined pricing and timing before I decide whether to proceed.
  14. Luis
    Exactly. I can arrange a 7 alongside the revised assessment process. Neither the technical outcome nor the commercial terms has been settled in this discussion.
  15. Hana
    Don't call it like-for-like in the note. That could imply equivalence before anyone compares the added machine's data.
  16. Luis
    I will avoid that 8. The note will identify a second machine with missing data, not an equivalent unit whose requirements we already know.
  17. Hana
    What can I tell the coordinator today? I need a next step, not a message saying you've rejected it or agreed to fit it.
  18. Luis
    Say the 9 needs product information, revised assessment, and quotation. Capacity, installation requirements, cost, and completion timing are still to be established.
  19. Hana
    I'll arrange the product documents through the team. My preferred price and date remain requests until the revised review establishes the terms.
  20. Luis
    Thank you. The 10 remains clear: this conversation records the request and the next review steps, not approval to add equipment or a promise of unchanged terms.

Answers and explanations
  1. scope addition Turn 2. Scope addition identifies the second machine as new work introduced after the original electrical agreement.
  2. supply requirement Turn 4. Supply requirement depends on the specific equipment information, not simply on both appliances being called espresso machines.
  3. equipment data Turn 6. Equipment data are the missing product-specific information required for the revised review, not guessed ratings or client testing.
  4. capacity assessment Turn 8. Capacity assessment remains necessary for the addition; the conversation does not establish available capacity or a suitable arrangement.
  5. price commitment Turn 10. Price commitment for the extra machine has not been made; Hana's wish to retain the original price remains a request.
  6. schedule impact Turn 12. Schedule impact of the added equipment remains unassessed, so the original completion date cannot be guaranteed for the change.
  7. revised quotation Turn 14. Revised quotation will address defined changed work after the necessary review; no new or unchanged price is established now.
  8. like-for-like claim Turn 16. Like-for-like claim is unsupported because the added machine's relevant data have not been supplied or compared.
  9. change request Turn 18. Change request remains open for data collection and revised review, rather than being automatically accepted or rejected.
  10. approval boundary Turn 20. Approval boundary keeps the added machine unapproved until the relevant technical and commercial process establishes what is acceptable.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Hana: We're adding a second espresso machine. Can the work still fit the original price and completion date, or does this need separate review?
  2. Luis: I understand the request, but it is a scope addition. The second machine was added after we agreed the electrical scope, and its equipment data are not available yet.
  3. Hana: I assumed another espresso machine would need similar provision. Is the category enough information, or do you need its specific details?
  4. Luis: The category does not establish the supply requirement. I cannot assume the added unit has the same relevant characteristics or that the existing provision is suitable.
  5. Hana: Which documents should the project team send? I don't want ratings guessed from a photograph or a general product name.
  6. Luis: We need the relevant equipment data for the added unit through the usual project process. This is an information request, not a request for you to test or connect anything.
  7. Hana: Once the data arrive, you'll review capacity and installation requirements? Neither answer is established for the addition yet?
  8. Luis: A capacity assessment forms part of the revised review. At present, capacity and installation requirements are not established, so I cannot give a yes-or-no technical approval.
  9. Hana: I'd like the original price retained, but record that as my request. You haven't quoted or accepted the extra work.
  10. Luis: Correct. There is no unchanged price commitment for the addition. We can preserve your preference without recording it as something we have already accepted.
  11. Hana: Please keep the original completion date in the review brief. Other work depends on it, although this change's effect is still unknown.
  12. Luis: I will include it, but the schedule impact remains open. I cannot promise the same completion date for changed work before the requirements and arrangements have been assessed.
  13. Hana: Then a technical answer won't settle everything. I need defined pricing and timing before I decide whether to proceed.
  14. Luis: Exactly. I can arrange a revised quotation alongside the revised assessment process. Neither the technical outcome nor the commercial terms has been settled in this discussion.
  15. Hana: Don't call it like-for-like in the note. That could imply equivalence before anyone compares the added machine's data.
  16. Luis: I will avoid that like-for-like claim. The note will identify a second machine with missing data, not an equivalent unit whose requirements we already know.
  17. Hana: What can I tell the coordinator today? I need a next step, not a message saying you've rejected it or agreed to fit it.
  18. Luis: Say the change request needs product information, revised assessment, and quotation. Capacity, installation requirements, cost, and completion timing are still to be established.
  19. Hana: I'll arrange the product documents through the team. My preferred price and date remain requests until the revised review establishes the terms.
  20. Luis: Thank you. The approval boundary remains clear: this conversation records the request and the next review steps, not approval to add equipment or a promise of unchanged terms.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A second machine changes the enquiry

An electrician discusses an added espresso machine with a cafe owner.

  1. Electrician: The second machine wasn't included in the equipment information for our agreed scope.
  2. Cafe owner: It looks like the first one. Can we keep the same price and opening date?
  3. Electrician: We need its manufacturer's data and proposed location to assess the added connected load.
  4. Cafe owner: I'll ask the supplier for those. Does its similar appearance tell you anything useful?
  5. Electrician: It identifies a possible model family, but doesn't establish demand or compatibility here.
  6. Cafe owner: Could you prepare a change quotation once the technical review has enough information?
  7. Electrician: Yes, with the proposed work and timing stated, rather than silently extending the existing scope.
  8. Cafe owner: Please show the difference clearly so I can decide whether to keep one machine initially.
  9. Electrician: I'll include that comparison without recommending an unassessed circuit arrangement for either option.
  10. Cafe owner: Then obtain the data first; my request for a price is not approval to proceed.
The alternate enclosure has different details

An electrician and supplier discuss a proposed product substitution.

  1. Electrician: Your alternate enclosure has the same external size, but the specification details differ.
  2. Supplier: It's available tomorrow, while the specified model is on a longer lead time.
  3. Electrician: Availability is useful, but it doesn't establish suitability. Please provide the full comparison.
  4. Supplier: Which details should I include so the reviewer can evaluate the proposed alternative?
  5. Electrician: Use the stated project requirements and both manufacturers' data, rather than a same-size description.
  6. Supplier: I can show each requirement against the offered product and identify any unconfirmed points.
  7. Electrician: Please retain the original model on the order until the substitution decision is recorded.
  8. Supplier: Understood. I won't treat the request for comparison as approval to dispatch the alternate.
  9. Electrician: The client also needs the quoted delivery difference separated from any installation-date prediction.
  10. Supplier: I'll provide the supply dates as quotations, with technical acceptance and site timing still separate.
A dedicated circuit is not spare capacity

An electrician explains terminology in a proposal to a facilities client.

  1. Electrician: The proposal mentions a dedicated circuit for the identified equipment in the reviewed design.
  2. Facilities client: Does dedicated mean we can add other appliances there whenever the office expands?
  3. Electrician: No. It describes an intended specific use, not a promise of spare capacity.
  4. Facilities client: I was reading it as an extra circuit reserved for any future equipment.
  5. Electrician: Then we should clarify the intended equipment reference in the proposal before you approve it.
  6. Facilities client: Please do. Our equipment schedule may change when the new tenant gives us its list.
  7. Electrician: That change needs review; we shouldn't assume the original assessment covers an unknown future list.
  8. Facilities client: Can the quotation identify which equipment information it was based on for that assessment?
  9. Electrician: Yes, I'll request that reference and keep future additions outside the current confirmation.
  10. Facilities client: That gives us a clear basis for discussing changes when the tenant's actual requirements arrive.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A second machine changes the question
  1. Read turns 1-10, separating a machine category from product-specific data.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20; distinguish requested price and date from commitments.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Request data before confirming terms

Complete the café update. Keep the missing information, changed scope, review route, and unresolved commercial terms visible.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Owner: "The second machine was added after scope ___."

Answer and explanation

A. agreement

The timing makes the second machine a new scope request.

2. Electrician: "Its equipment ___ are not available."

Answer and explanation

D. data

Product-specific data are missing and needed for the revised review.

3. Owner: "We need revised assessment and a ___."

Answer and explanation

B. quotation

The added work needs pricing through a revised quotation process.

4. Electrician: "Capacity, price, and timing are not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

D. established

None of these outcomes has been determined for the unassessed addition.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Course: Electricians' Workplace English
Professional audience: Electricians, electrical apprentices in supervised communication roles, electrical crew leads, and service coordinators
Scope: The cases and products are fictional. This book teaches English, not electrical testing, switching, isolation, diagnosis, installation, circuit identification, or code compliance. Follow actual training, authorization, procedures, manufacturer information, and applicable requirements. Client descriptions, labels, outage plans, and handover records do not prove equipment is de-energized or safe to use or work on. Lesson 07: Assessing added equipment and substitutions
Original fictional case: After electrical scope is agreed for a café, owner Hana adds a second espresso machine and asks electrician Luis to include it at the original price and completion date. Equipment data for the added machine are unavailable. Luis can request the data and arrange a revised assessment and quotation. Electrical capacity and installation requirements are not established. The discussion must not assume the new machine matches the first, fits the existing provision, or can be added without cost or schedule effects.
Language workshop: A second machine changes the question
Communication goal: Request missing equipment information and explain the need for revised assessment and quotation without confirming capacity, price, or timing.
Language guidance: The second machine is a scope addition, not an already assessed part of the original work. Equipment data are missing, so capacity and installation requirements remain unknown. The machine category alone does not establish that it matches the first unit.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): You want to add a second espresso machine. / That machine was added after the electrical scope was agreed. / We do not have the equipment data for the added unit. / Please provide the relevant product information through the usual project process. / I cannot assume it has the same requirements as the first machine. / Available capacity has not been established for the addition. / The installation requirements still need assessment. / I can arrange a revised assessment and quotation. / I understand you want to keep the original price. / I cannot confirm unchanged cost for the added machine. / I understand the original completion date matters to you. / I cannot promise the same date before the change is assessed. / Technical suitability and commercial terms both remain open. / This information request does not authorize connection or testing. / Second machine requested; data missing; capacity, requirements, price, and timing unresolved. / We need the data for the revised assessment and quotation.
Vocabulary: equipment data: Product-specific technical information needed for assessment. / espresso machine: Coffee-making equipment forming the added request in this case. / additional load: Extra electrical demand associated with added equipment. / rated input: Manufacturer-stated input requirement under specified conditions. / nameplate: Product marking showing identification and relevant ratings. / manufacturer specification: Technical requirements or attributes supplied by the manufacturer. / supply requirement: Electrical supply characteristics required by the equipment. / capacity assessment: Evaluation of whether the relevant provision can meet the proposed demand. / existing provision: Electrical arrangement already included or available for the project. / installation requirement: Condition or detail needed for the specified installation. / revised assessment: Updated review accounting for changed information or scope. / revised quotation: Updated price proposal for the changed defined work. / original scope: Work included before the new request. / scope addition: Extra item proposed after the agreed scope. / active power: Electrical power associated with net energy transfer, expressed in watts or kilowatts. / price commitment: Agreed promise about cost. / schedule impact: Effect of a change on timing. / technical suitability: Whether the proposed arrangement meets relevant technical needs. / product substitution: Replacement of a specified item with a different product. / like-for-like claim: Assertion that a replacement or addition has equivalent relevant characteristics. / apparent power: For a single-phase load, RMS voltage times RMS current, expressed in volt-amperes. / approval boundary: Limit of what has actually been authorized. / change request: Proposal to alter the agreed work or requirements. / assessment outcome: Result reached after the required review.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Handing over electrical recordsAn old room name needs verification+

Your goal

Confirm document receipt, record an identification discrepancy, and assign a dated follow-up without relabeling or implying operational clearance.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

An old room name needs verification

At an office handover, facilities contact Ben receives the as-built drawings from electrical lead Priya. One circuit-directory entry still says old store, although the room is now called archive. The associated circuit identification needs verification; the room-name change does not justify relabeling by assumption. Priya accepts follow-up and will report Thursday. No operational clearance is inferred from the handover, and no verification result or corrected entry is supplied.

Ben: Facilities contact / Priya: Electrical lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What has Ben received?

Answer and explanation

B. The as-built drawings

The as-built drawings are received, while the directory question remains unresolved.

2. What discrepancy remains?

Answer and explanation

C. The directory says old store while the room is now archive

The mismatch concerns the old directory wording and the current room name.

3. What does Priya accept?

Answer and explanation

D. Follow-up and a Thursday report, without an invented verification outcome

Priya owns the follow-up and report, but no verified identification or correction is established.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The room is now called archive, and the directory entry says old store. That wording difference does not verify which circuit serves the room. Priya must follow up through the appropriate process; no relabeling is authorized simply by matching room names.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
as-built drawing
Record drawing intended to represent the installed arrangement, subject to its actual verification status.receive the as-built drawings
document handover
Transfer of project records to the receiving party.complete the document handover
circuit directory
List intended to identify circuits and the areas or equipment they serve.verify the circuit directory
room designation
Name or identifier assigned to a room.confirm the current room designation
legacy label
Older wording retained in a current record or label.identify a legacy label
identification discrepancy
Mismatch or uncertainty in how an item or circuit is identified.record an identification discrepancy
verification task
Defined check needed to establish the relevant information.assign the verification task
relabeling
Changing identification wording on a label or record.avoid relabeling by assumption
record correction
Amendment based on verified information and the actual process.arrange a record correction
document receipt
Confirmation that records have been supplied to the recipient.acknowledge document receipt
follow-up owner
Person responsible for the next action on an open item.name the follow-up owner
reporting date
Day committed for an update or report.confirm the reporting date
verification outcome
Result of the relevant identification check.await the verification outcome
operational clearance
Authorization or verified status for use under the actual process.distinguish operational clearance
handover exception
Open item retained when other handover elements are complete.list a handover exception
record accuracy
Correctness of the information contained in a document or label.verify record accuracy
installed arrangement
Actual configuration of the relevant equipment or system.distinguish the installed arrangement
power factor
Ratio of active power to apparent power, distinct from output-to-input efficiency.interpret power factor
RMS
Root mean square; an effective-value measure used for AC voltage and current.state the RMS basis
circuit identity
Verified correspondence between a circuit and what it serves.establish circuit identity
update commitment
Promise to provide further information at a stated time.make an update commitment
open-item record
List or note preserving unresolved matters.maintain the open-item record
approved amendment
Change accepted through the applicable process.confirm an approved amendment
handover readback
Repetition of received records and unresolved items to confirm understanding.give a handover readback

The as-built drawings are received, but their receipt does not close the identification question or establish operational clearance. Thursday is the committed reporting date, not a guaranteed verification outcome, correction date, or authorization to operate equipment.

C Notice the language

Document receipt is not verification of every detail

A handover may contain received records and a remaining identification question. Acknowledge the documents, preserve the exact discrepancy, and name the owner and reporting date. Do not let a familiar room name substitute for verification of the electrical identification.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the records
You have received the as-built drawings.
Name the remaining issue
One circuit-directory entry still says old store.
State the current room name
The room is now called archive.
Preserve the uncertainty
The circuit identification still needs verification.
Avoid a naming shortcut
A room-name change does not prove the circuit identity.
Avoid premature relabeling
We should not relabel the entry by assumption.
Accept follow-up
I will take responsibility for the follow-up.
Name the owner
Priya owns the identification follow-up.
State the commitment
I will report on Thursday.
Keep the outcome open
I cannot state the verification result before it is established.
Separate receipt and accuracy
Receiving the drawings does not verify every directory entry.
Keep the exception visible
The identification discrepancy remains an open handover item.
Avoid operational inference
This handover does not establish operational clearance.
Preserve the actual process
Any correction needs verified information and the relevant approval process.
Read back the handover
Drawings received; old-store entry unresolved; Priya follows up and reports Thursday.
Close with the right limit
The reporting date is committed, but no corrected identity is supplied here.
6 language and precision notes
As-built
A record-document description, not a reason to ignore an identified discrepancy.
Still says
Flags older wording without proving the correct electrical identity.
Now called
Establishes the current room name, not the circuit correspondence.
Will report Thursday
Commits to communication, not a predetermined technical result.
By assumption
Describes the shortcut the dialogue must avoid.
Received versus verified
Receipt of records and verification of their contents are separate facts.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which handover statement is accurate?

Answer and explanation

A. Drawings received; directory wording differs from the room name; identification needs verification.

The statement preserves document receipt and the separate unresolved identification question.

2. Why is immediate relabeling unsupported?

Answer and explanation

A. The current room name does not establish circuit identity.

Verification is required because matching a name does not prove the circuit correspondence.

3. What does Thursday identify?

Answer and explanation

A. Priya's committed reporting date

Priya promises to report Thursday without promising an unestablished technical outcome.

4. What belongs in the open-item record?

Answer and explanation

C. Old-store entry, current archive name, verification needed, Priya as owner, Thursday report

These details preserve the discrepancy, required review, responsible person, and communication commitment.

E Conversations

An old room name needs verification

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ben: Facilities contact / Priya: Electrical lead

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • identification discrepancy
  • legacy label
  • Document receipt
  • handover readback
  • handover exception
  • follow-up owner
  • record correction
  • reporting date
  • operational clearance
  • circuit identity
  1. Ben
    I've received the as-built drawings, Priya. One directory entry says old store, though the room is now archive. Can we keep that open?
  2. Priya
    I will keep that 1 open. The room name has changed, but the associated circuit identification still needs verification rather than a label change based on the name alone.
  3. Ben
    I was about to suggest changing the words. That would assume the entry really identifies the room we're discussing, wouldn't it?
  4. Priya
    Exactly. The 2 is the unresolved point. We should not treat a familiar location name as proof of the electrical correspondence.
  5. Ben
    Does receiving the as-built set establish that this entry was checked? I don't want document delivery confused with a verification result.
  6. Priya
    3 confirms that you have the drawings. It does not establish that this directory entry is verified or that the discrepancy has been resolved.
  7. Ben
    Please retain old store exactly, alongside archive. The follow-up needs the original wording, not a silently amended entry.
  8. Priya
    I will record the 4 as old store and the current designation as archive. The record will not silently replace one with the other as though verification were complete.
  9. Ben
    Who owns the follow-up? I want a name so facilities and electrical aren't each expecting the other to respond.
  10. Priya
    I accept it as the 5. Please record Priya against the item. That identifies who is responsible without pretending the check has already produced a result.
  11. Ben
    When will you report back? We need a definite update point even if the result isn't established by then.
  12. Priya
    I will report Thursday. That is the 6 I am committing to, not a promise that a particular circuit identity or amendment has already been established.
  13. Ben
    Then Thursday is a report, not a promised corrected label or permission to operate equipment under an assumed new identity.
  14. Priya
    Correct. No 7 is inferred from this handover. The records discussion does not authorize operating equipment or relying on an unverified label.
  15. Ben
    Any correction needs verified information and the project process. The received drawings and unresolved circuit identification remain separate statuses.
  16. Priya
    Yes. A 8 should reflect verified information and the relevant approval process. It should not be a guessed amendment made just to make the wording look current.
  17. Ben
    Can you read the whole entry back? Include the old wording, current name, required verification, your ownership, and the reporting date.
  18. Priya
    The 9 is: as-built drawings received; old-store entry differs from current archive designation; identification needs verification; Priya owns follow-up and will report Thursday.
  19. Ben
    That's accurate. We'll acknowledge the drawings while keeping identification open until the follow-up actually supports closure.
  20. Priya
    I will retain the 10 until the actual outcome supports closure. No corrected circuit identity, relabeling approval, or operational status is established by this conversation.

Answers and explanations
  1. identification discrepancy Turn 2. Identification discrepancy is the old-store directory wording against the current archive name, with circuit correspondence still unverified.
  2. circuit identity Turn 4. Circuit identity requires verification; the room-name change does not establish which circuit serves the archive.
  3. Document receipt Turn 6. Document receipt confirms the as-built drawings reached Ben, not that every directory entry or circuit identity has been verified.
  4. legacy label Turn 8. Legacy label is the exact wording old store, retained alongside archive so the specific entry can be followed up.
  5. follow-up owner Turn 10. Follow-up owner is Priya, who explicitly accepts responsibility for the unresolved identification issue.
  6. reporting date Turn 12. Reporting date is Thursday for Priya's update, not a guaranteed correction or a predetermined verification result.
  7. operational clearance Turn 14. Operational clearance is not established by receiving drawings, discussing names, or assigning a reporting date.
  8. record correction Turn 16. Record correction needs verified information and the applicable process; it is not authorized by a room-name assumption.
  9. handover readback Turn 18. Handover readback combines received drawings, the unresolved label discrepancy, Priya's ownership, and the Thursday report without adding clearance.
  10. handover exception Turn 20. Handover exception keeps the unverified directory entry open while allowing receipt of the drawings to be acknowledged separately.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ben: I've received the as-built drawings, Priya. One directory entry says old store, though the room is now archive. Can we keep that open?
  2. Priya: I will keep that identification discrepancy open. The room name has changed, but the associated circuit identification still needs verification rather than a label change based on the name alone.
  3. Ben: I was about to suggest changing the words. That would assume the entry really identifies the room we're discussing, wouldn't it?
  4. Priya: Exactly. The circuit identity is the unresolved point. We should not treat a familiar location name as proof of the electrical correspondence.
  5. Ben: Does receiving the as-built set establish that this entry was checked? I don't want document delivery confused with a verification result.
  6. Priya: Document receipt confirms that you have the drawings. It does not establish that this directory entry is verified or that the discrepancy has been resolved.
  7. Ben: Please retain old store exactly, alongside archive. The follow-up needs the original wording, not a silently amended entry.
  8. Priya: I will record the legacy label as old store and the current designation as archive. The record will not silently replace one with the other as though verification were complete.
  9. Ben: Who owns the follow-up? I want a name so facilities and electrical aren't each expecting the other to respond.
  10. Priya: I accept it as the follow-up owner. Please record Priya against the item. That identifies who is responsible without pretending the check has already produced a result.
  11. Ben: When will you report back? We need a definite update point even if the result isn't established by then.
  12. Priya: I will report Thursday. That is the reporting date I am committing to, not a promise that a particular circuit identity or amendment has already been established.
  13. Ben: Then Thursday is a report, not a promised corrected label or permission to operate equipment under an assumed new identity.
  14. Priya: Correct. No operational clearance is inferred from this handover. The records discussion does not authorize operating equipment or relying on an unverified label.
  15. Ben: Any correction needs verified information and the project process. The received drawings and unresolved circuit identification remain separate statuses.
  16. Priya: Yes. A record correction should reflect verified information and the relevant approval process. It should not be a guessed amendment made just to make the wording look current.
  17. Ben: Can you read the whole entry back? Include the old wording, current name, required verification, your ownership, and the reporting date.
  18. Priya: The handover readback is: as-built drawings received; old-store entry differs from current archive designation; identification needs verification; Priya owns follow-up and will report Thursday.
  19. Ben: That's accurate. We'll acknowledge the drawings while keeping identification open until the follow-up actually supports closure.
  20. Priya: I will retain the handover exception until the actual outcome supports closure. No corrected circuit identity, relabeling approval, or operational status is established by this conversation.
Dialogue in the book, page 73 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The archive still says old store

An electrician hands over documents to the facilities client.

  1. Electrician: The as-built drawings are received, but the circuit directory's old-store entry remains unresolved.
  2. Facilities client: That room is now the archive. Can we assume it's just the old name?
  3. Electrician: It may be, but the circuit identification needs verification before that conclusion is recorded.
  4. Facilities client: Who will handle that question after your crew leaves this part of the project?
  5. Electrician: Priya has accepted the follow-up and will report Thursday, with the query still visible.
  6. Facilities client: Please distinguish that from a promise that the directory will be corrected by Thursday.
  7. Electrician: Yes, Thursday is the report date; the outcome depends on the verification.
  8. Facilities client: Does receiving the drawings establish that every other technical check is complete?
  9. Electrician: No. Document receipt and technical verification have separate statuses in the handover record.
  10. Facilities client: I'll retain that distinction and expect Priya's report on the unresolved archive reference.
A record belongs to another asset

An electrician checks a commissioning document with a building representative.

  1. Electrician: This commissioning record identifies board B, but the handover folder is labeled board D.
  2. Building representative: Could the folder name simply be wrong, or does the report need reissuing?
  3. Electrician: We need to reconcile the references before deciding which record or label is incorrect.
  4. Building representative: Please don't remove either reference yet; our consultant may need to compare the originals.
  5. Electrician: Agreed. I'll flag the mismatch and request confirmation from the document author.
  6. Building representative: Is the document's presence enough to mark board D's record requirement as complete?
  7. Electrician: Not while its applicability remains unresolved. Received and matched are different statuses.
  8. Building representative: I'll keep the document item open and name the reconciliation owner in the register.
  9. Electrician: The author can respond Tuesday. I'll ask for a confirmed reference, not an informal guess.
  10. Building representative: Please include that response in the final pack so future readers understand the correction.
Handover does not replace user orientation

An electrician clarifies a client's request for a building-team briefing.

  1. Electrician: The document handover is booked Monday. You also mentioned a briefing for the building team.
  2. Client: Yes, but the team cannot attend Monday. Can the paperwork stand in for the briefing?
  3. Electrician: The pack gives reference information; it doesn't confirm that a requested orientation has happened.
  4. Client: Then let's keep the briefing as an outstanding item rather than mark everything delivered.
  5. Electrician: Which staff need the session, and who can confirm a suitable time for them?
  6. Client: Our facilities supervisor can coordinate attendance. Please discuss dates with her directly this afternoon.
  7. Electrician: I'll ask the appropriate lead to agree the session scope and timing with her.
  8. Client: Please avoid giving operating instructions through an informal call to whoever answers the phone.
  9. Electrician: Agreed. This handover will identify the documents and the arranged briefing, not improvise technical training.
  10. Client: That gives us an honest record of what's received and what still needs scheduling.

REFERENCE
Course: Electricians' Workplace English
Professional audience: Electricians, electrical apprentices in supervised communication roles, electrical crew leads, and service coordinators
Scope: The cases and products are fictional. This book teaches English, not electrical testing, switching, isolation, diagnosis, installation, circuit identification, or code compliance. Follow actual training, authorization, procedures, manufacturer information, and applicable requirements. Client descriptions, labels, outage plans, and handover records do not prove equipment is de-energized or safe to use or work on. These dialogues authorize no electrical operation, relabeling, routing change, substitution, or added load.
Lesson 08: Handing over electrical records
Original fictional case: At an office handover, facilities contact Ben receives the as-built drawings from electrical lead Priya. One circuit-directory entry still says old store, although the room is now called archive. The associated circuit identification needs verification; the room-name change does not justify relabeling by assumption. Priya accepts follow-up and will report Thursday. No operational clearance is inferred from the handover, and no verification result or corrected entry is supplied.
Language workshop: An old room name needs verification
Communication goal: Confirm document receipt, record an identification discrepancy, and assign a dated follow-up without relabeling or implying operational clearance.
Language guidance: The room is now called archive, and the directory entry says old store. That wording difference does not verify which circuit serves the room. Priya must follow up through the appropriate process; no relabeling is authorized simply by matching room names.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): You have received the as-built drawings. / One circuit-directory entry still says old store. / The room is now called archive. / The circuit identification still needs verification. / A room-name change does not prove the circuit identity. / We should not relabel the entry by assumption. / I will take responsibility for the follow-up. / Priya owns the identification follow-up. / I will report on Thursday. / I cannot state the verification result before it is established. / Receiving the drawings does not verify every directory entry. / The identification discrepancy remains an open handover item. / This handover does not establish operational clearance. / Any correction needs verified information and the relevant approval process. / Drawings received; old-store entry unresolved; Priya follows up and reports Thursday. / The reporting date is committed, but no corrected identity is supplied here.
Vocabulary: as-built drawing: Record drawing intended to represent the installed arrangement, subject to its actual verification status. / document handover: Transfer of project records to the receiving party. / circuit directory: List intended to identify circuits and the areas or equipment they serve. / room designation: Name or identifier assigned to a room. / legacy label: Older wording retained in a current record or label. / identification discrepancy: Mismatch or uncertainty in how an item or circuit is identified. / verification task: Defined check needed to establish the relevant information. / relabeling: Changing identification wording on a label or record. / record correction: Amendment based on verified information and the actual process. / document receipt: Confirmation that records have been supplied to the recipient. / follow-up owner: Person responsible for the next action on an open item. / reporting date: Day committed for an update or report. / verification outcome: Result of the relevant identification check. / operational clearance: Authorization or verified status for use under the actual process. / handover exception: Open item retained when other handover elements are complete. / record accuracy: Correctness of the information contained in a document or label. / installed arrangement: Actual configuration of the relevant equipment or system. / power factor: Ratio of active power to apparent power, distinct from output-to-input efficiency. / RMS: Root mean square; an effective-value measure used for AC voltage and current. / circuit identity: Verified correspondence between a circuit and what it serves. / update commitment: Promise to provide further information at a stated time. / open-item record: List or note preserving unresolved matters. / approved amendment: Change accepted through the applicable process. / handover readback: Repetition of received records and unresolved items to confirm understanding.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10; distinguish old store, archive, and unverified circuit identity. Switch roles for turns 11-20; make Thursday a committed report, not a promised correction. Check and read the transfer while keeping document receipt separate from operational clearance.
Partner: Ben: Facilities contact / Priya: Electrical lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the handover record. Retain the current and old names, the verification need, the owner, and the committed report date.
